Founding of the United States

This article is about political and social developments, and the origin and aftermath of the war. For military actions, see [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War"). For other uses, see [American Revolution (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution_(disambiguation) "American Revolution (disambiguation)").

**Part of the [Atlantic Revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Revolutions "Atlantic Revolutions"), <br> and the [Age of Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Revolution "Age of Revolution")**

American Revolution

The **American Revolution** (1765–1789) was a [political movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_movement "Political movement") in the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies "Thirteen Colonies") of [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain "Kingdom of Great Britain"). It began as a colonial [rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebellion "Rebellion") and evolved into a [revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution "Revolution"), the result of which was the creation of the United States following the colonies' victory in the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War") (1775–1783) and British recognition of their sovereign independence in the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1783) "Treaty of Paris (1783)"). The [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress "Second Continental Congress"), as the [provisional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_government "Provisional government"), established the [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army "Continental Army") and appointed [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington") as its [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commanding_General_of_the_United_States_Army "Commanding General of the United States Army") in 1775. In May 1776, Congress voted to suppress all forms of [Crown authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown "The Crown"), to be replaced by locally created authority, and each state created a constitution. The Congress passed the [Lee Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Resolution "Lee Resolution") for independence on July 2 and unanimously adopted the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence") on [July 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_of_July "Fourth of July") arguing for the separation based on ["inalienable rights"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law"). The individual member states together created a [shared sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared_sovereignty "Shared sovereignty") under the [Articles of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation "Articles of Confederation") to facilitate international affairs and the promulgation of the war. Throughout most of the conflict the outcome appeared uncertain, but a decisive 1781 victory by combined French and Continental Army forces commanded by Washington in the [Siege of Yorktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Yorktown "Siege of Yorktown") led King [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III") and the [Fox–North coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox%E2%80%93North_coalition "Fox–North coalition") in government to negotiate the cessation of [British colonial rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_the_United_States "Colonial history of the United States"), and the acknowledgment of American sovereignty. A more centralized federal republic was created with the passage of the [Constitution of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States "Constitution of the United States") that took effect in 1789, and the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights "United States Bill of Rights") was [ratified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratification "Ratification") in 1791.

Discontent with British colonial rule began shortly after the end of the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War "French and Indian War") in 1763. Although the Thirteen Colonies had fought and supported the war directly, the [British Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Great_Britain "Parliament of Great Britain") imposed new taxes on the colonists, ostensibly to compensate for wartime costs, and transferred control of the colonies' western lands to British officials in [Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal "Montreal"). In 1765, Representatives from several colonies held the [Stamp Act Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_Act_Congress "Stamp Act Congress") in New York City, resulting in the [Declaration of Rights and Grievances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Rights_and_Grievances "Declaration of Rights and Grievances"), which argued that [taxation without representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_without_representation "Taxation without representation"), among other British policies, violated their [rights as Englishmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_of_Englishmen "Rights of Englishmen"). The [Stamp Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_Act_1765 "Stamp Act 1765") was repealed in 1767, but tensions increased again following Parliament's passage of the [Townshend Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Townshend_Acts "Townshend Acts"). In an effort to quell the mounting rebellion, King George III deployed [British troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Army_during_the_American_Revolutionary_War "British Army during the American Revolutionary War") to the colony of [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Massachusetts_Bay "Province of Massachusetts Bay"), which resulted in the [Boston Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Massacre "Boston Massacre") in 1770. In December 1773, a local faction of the [Sons of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty "Sons of Liberty") orchestrated the [Boston Tea Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Tea_Party "Boston Tea Party"), during which they dumped chests of taxed tea owned by the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company "East India Company") into [Boston Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Harbor "Boston Harbor"). Parliament responded by enacting a [series of punitive laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intolerable_Acts "Intolerable Acts"), called the Intolerable Acts. These were intended to end self-government in Massachusetts, but instead increased American support for revolution.

In 1774, twelve of the Thirteen Colonies sent delegates to the [First Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Continental_Congress "First Continental Congress") (the [Province of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Georgia "Province of Georgia") joined in 1775) in order to begin coordinating [Patriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_(American_Revolution) "Patriot (American Revolution)") resistance. In August 1775, King George III [proclaimed Massachusetts to be in rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Rebellion "Proclamation of Rebellion"). The British subsequently attempted to disarm the colonists, resulting in the [Battles of Lexington and Concord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_Lexington_and_Concord "Battles of Lexington and Concord") and starting the Revolutionary War. The [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress "Second Continental Congress") convened in May 1775 and created the Continental Army, which soon [laid siege to Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Boston "Siege of Boston") and forced the British to withdraw by sea in March 1776. On July 2, the Congress passed the [Lee Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Resolution "Lee Resolution"), affirming their support for joint independence. On July 4, 1776, they unanimously adopted the Declaration of Independence.

The Revolutionary War continued for another seven years, with [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "France in the American Revolutionary War") and [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_and_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Spain and the American Revolutionary War") eventually entering. On September 28, 1781, Washington commanded the Continental Army's capture of a British army under [General Cornwallis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Cornwallis,_1st_Marquess_Cornwallis "Charles Cornwallis, 1st Marquess Cornwallis") at the [Siege of Yorktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Yorktown "Siege of Yorktown"), leading to the collapse of King George's control of Parliament. Consensus in Parliament soon shifted to the war ending on American terms. On September 3, 1783, the British signed the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1783) "Treaty of Paris (1783)"), recognizing the sovereign independence of the United States, and ceding to the new nation nearly all the territory east of the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River "Mississippi River") and south of the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes "Great Lakes"). The United States became the first nation to establish a federal republic with a written constitution based on the principles of [universal natural rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law"), [consent of the governed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed "Consent of the governed"), and [equality under the law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_under_the_law "Equality under the law"), although with significant democratic limitations compared to later evolution of the concept.

## Origins

Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence "Wikipedia:Verifiability")._(September 2025)__([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal "Help:Maintenance template removal"))_ |

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_American_Revolution "Timeline of the American Revolution").

Further information: [Colonial history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_the_United_States "Colonial history of the United States")

See also: [List of 18th-century wars involving the Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_18th-century_wars_involving_the_Thirteen_Colonies "List of 18th-century wars involving the Thirteen Colonies")

After the [Glorious Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glorious_Revolution "Glorious Revolution") in [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England "Kingdom of England"), the British had adopted a strict trade policy of " [salutary neglect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salutary_neglect "Salutary neglect")," whereby the Thirteen Colonies were largely left to govern themselves.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200530-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200530) As a result of this new policy, as well as the ideals of [liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty "Liberty") which had been borne out of the Glorious Revolution, there emerged new government systems (as exemplified by [William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn "William Penn")'s [Frame of Government of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frame_of_Government_of_Pennsylvania "Frame of Government of Pennsylvania") and the [Massachusetts Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Charter "Massachusetts Charter") of 1691); religious institutions (as exemplified by the democratic nature of [Congregational Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregationalism_in_the_United_States "Congregationalism in the United States") in New England and the [First Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Great_Awakening "First Great Awakening") across the colonies); and occasionally, [attitudes towards slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States#In_Colonial_America "Abolitionism in the United States") (as [exemplified by Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_Massachusetts#Law_1641%E2%80%931703 "History of slavery in Massachusetts") and, [at least initially, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Experiment "Georgia Experiment") banning the practice), although the latter of these was less common. This British policy reverted significantly after the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War "French and Indian War"). The British political establishment, especially after taking massive swathes of land from the former territories of [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France "New France"), pursued a policy of greater control over colonial affairs, prompting the Thirteen Colonies to seek restoration of greater autonomy from Britain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-2-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-2) One Patriot, Capt. Levi Preston of [Danvers, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danvers,_Massachusetts "Danvers, Massachusetts"), was asked why the Americans rebelled against England, and he responded: "...we always had governed ourselves, and we always meant to. They didn't mean we should."[&#8202;](#ref-back-3-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-3)

### 1651–1763: Evolution of colonial policy

Further information: [Colonial history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_the_United_States "Colonial history of the United States")

#### Development of colonial self-government

Further information: [Colonial government in the Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_government_in_the_Thirteen_Colonies "Colonial government in the Thirteen Colonies")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Map_of_territorial_growth_1775.svg/250px-Map_of_territorial_growth_1775.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map_of_territorial_growth_1775.svg) A 1775 map of Eastern North America, including the [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_(1763%E2%80%931791) "Province of Quebec (1763–1791)"), the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies "Thirteen Colonies") on the Atlantic Coast, and the [Indian Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Reserve_(1763) "Indian Reserve (1763)") as defined by the [Royal Proclamation of 1763](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763 "Royal Proclamation of 1763"). The border between the red and pink areas represents the 1763 [Proclamation line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_line "Proclamation line"), and the orange area represents [Spanish colonial claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas "Spanish colonization of the Americas").

The [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies "Thirteen Colonies") were established in the 17th century originally as part of the [English Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Empire "English Empire") and later as part of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire") after the [union of England and Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_England_and_Scotland "Union of England and Scotland") in 1707.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201617-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201617)

The development of a unique American identity can be traced back to the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_overseas_possessions_in_the_Wars_of_the_Three_Kingdoms "English overseas possessions in the Wars of the Three Kingdoms") (1642–1651) and its aftermath. In that period the [Puritan colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Puritans_in_North_America "History of the Puritans in North America") of [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Colonies "New England Colonies") supported the [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_England "Commonwealth of England") government responsible for the [execution of King Charles I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_King_Charles_I "Execution of King Charles I"). After the [Stuart Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart_Restoration "Stuart Restoration") of 1660, [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Bay_Colony "Massachusetts Bay Colony") did not recognize [Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England "Charles II of England") as the legitimate king for more than a year after his [coronation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_British_monarch "Coronation of the British monarch").[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-5) In [King Philip's War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Philip's_War "King Philip's War") (1675–1678), the New England colonies fought a coalition of a few American Indian tribes without military assistance from England, thereby contributing to the development of a uniquely [American identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States "Culture of the United States") separate from that of the [British identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_Kingdom "Culture of the United Kingdom").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELepore19995–7-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELepore19995–7)

In the 1680s, Charles and his brother, [James II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_II_of_England "James II of England"), attempted to bring New England under direct English control.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENettels1938297-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENettels1938297) The colonists fiercely opposed this, and [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown "The Crown") nullified their [colonial charters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_charters_in_the_Thirteen_Colonies "Colonial charters in the Thirteen Colonies") in response.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELovejoy1987148–156,_155–157,_169–170-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELovejoy1987148–156,_155–157,_169–170) In 1686, James finalized these efforts by consolidating the separate New England colonies along with [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_York "Province of New York") and [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey "Province of New Jersey") into the [Dominion of New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_England "Dominion of New England"). [Edmund Andros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Andros "Edmund Andros") was appointed royal governor and tasked with governing the new Dominion under his [direct rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_rule "Direct rule"). Colonial assemblies and [town meetings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_meeting "Town meeting") were restricted, new local taxes were levied, and rights were abridged. Dominion rule triggered bitter resentment throughout New England.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBarnes1960169–170-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBarnes1960169–170) When James tried to rule without [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_England "Parliament of England"), the English aristocracy removed him from power in the Glorious Revolution of 1688.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201612-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201612) This was followed by the [1689 Boston revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1689_Boston_revolt "1689 Boston revolt"), which overthrew Dominion rule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWebb1998190–191-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWebb1998190–191)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELustig2002201-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELustig2002201) Colonial governments reasserted their control after the revolt. The new monarchs, [William](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_III_of_England "William III of England") and [Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_II "Mary II"), granted new charters to the individual New England colonies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPalfrey1864596-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPalfrey1864596)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEEvans1922430-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEEvans1922430)

After the Glorious Revolution, the British Empire became a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy "Constitutional monarchy") with sovereignty in the [King-in-Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King-in-Parliament "King-in-Parliament"). [Aristocrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristocrat "Aristocrat") inherited seats in the [House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Lords "House of Lords"), while the [gentry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentry "Gentry") and merchants controlled the elected [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Great_Britain "House of Commons of Great Britain"). The king ruled through [cabinet ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_Kingdom "Cabinet of the United Kingdom") who depended on majority support in the Commons to govern effectively. [British subjects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_subject "British subject") on both sides of the Atlantic proudly claimed that the unwritten [British constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_constitution "British constitution"), with its guarantees of the [rights of Englishmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_of_Englishmen "Rights of Englishmen"), protected personal liberty better than any other government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201612–13_&_32-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201612–13_&_32)

The British constitution served as the model for [colonial governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_government_in_the_Thirteen_Colonies "Colonial government in the Thirteen Colonies"). The Crown appointed a [royal governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_government_in_the_Thirteen_Colonies#Governor "Colonial government in the Thirteen Colonies") in each colony to exercise [executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_(government) "Executive (government)") power, usually also appointing a [Governor's Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_government_in_the_Thirteen_Colonies#Governor's_Council "Colonial government in the Thirteen Colonies") largely to act as an unelected [upper chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house "Upper house") of the legislature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200546-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200546) [Property owners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_qualification "Property qualification") elected a colonial assembly amongst themselves with powers to legislate and levy taxes, but the British government reserved the right to [veto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veto "Veto") colonial legislation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201612–13-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201612–13)

The British government lacked the resources and information necessary for close supervision of the colonies. Instead, British officials negotiated and compromised with colonial leaders to gain compliance with imperial policies. The colonies defended themselves with [provincial troops and colonial militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_American_military_history "Colonial American military history") of locals, and the British government rarely sent military forces to America before 1755.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201631–32-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201631–32) According to historian [Robert Middlekauff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Middlekauff "Robert Middlekauff"), "Americans had become almost completely self-governing" before the American Revolution, a practice that was consistent with the British monarchy's practice of [salutary neglect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salutary_neglect "Salutary neglect").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200530-1)[\[1\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200530)

#### Mercantilism

Main article: [Mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism "Mercantilism")

British colonial policy was based on mercantilist theory, which held that Britain's national security depended on building a self-sufficient empire and controlling key trade routes. The purpose of colonies was to produce raw materials for Britain's use, consume British manufactures, and support the growth of British shipping. In the 1600s, the English Parliament enacted a series of laws called the [Navigation Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navigation_Acts "Navigation Acts") to ensure that the colonies fulfilled these purposes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiller19594-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiller19594)

The Navigation Acts restricted colonial trade with foreign countries. The Thirteen Colonies could trade with the rest of the empire but only ship certain commodities like tobacco to Britain. Any European imports bound for British America had to first pass through an English port and pay customs duties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201623-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201623) Other laws regulated developing colonial industries to prevent competition with British industries, such as the [Wool Act 1698](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool_Act_1698 "Wool Act 1698"), the [Hat Act 1731](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hat_Act_1731 "Hat Act 1731"), and the [Iron Act 1750](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Act_1750 "Iron Act 1750").[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-22) With little industry except shipbuilding, the colonies exported agricultural products in return for manufactured goods. They also imported molasses, rum, and sugar from the [British West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_West_Indies "British West Indies").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201619_&_23-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201619_&_23)

Colonial reactions to these policies were mixed. The [Molasses Act 1733](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molasses_Act_1733 "Molasses Act 1733"), for example, placed a duty of six [pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_British_penny_(1714%E2%80%931901) "History of the British penny (1714–1901)") per gallon upon foreign molasses imported into the colonies. This act was particularly egregious to the New England colonists, who protested it as unconstitutional taxation without representation in the British parliament. The act increased the smuggling of foreign molasses, and the British government ceased enforcement efforts after the 1740s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiller195998–99-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiller195998–99)

On the other hand, certain merchants and local industries benefited from the restrictions on foreign competition. The limits on foreign-built ships greatly benefited the colonial shipbuilding industry, particularly in New England.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEThomas1964632-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEThomas1964632) Some argue that the economic impact was minimal on the colonists,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWhaples1995140-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWhaples1995140)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEThomas1964-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEThomas1964) but the political friction that the acts triggered was more serious, as the merchants most directly affected were also the most politically active.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWalton1971-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWalton1971)

#### British military presence after 1754

During the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War "French and Indian War") (1754–1763), the British government fielded 45,000 soldiers, half [British Regulars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Regulars "British Regulars") and half [colonial volunteers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_troops_in_the_French_and_Indian_Wars "Provincial troops in the French and Indian Wars"). The colonies also contributed funds and materiel to the war effort; though, two-fifths of this spending was reimbursed by the British government. Great Britain defeated France and acquired [that nation's territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France "New France") east of the Mississippi River.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201645_&_47-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201645_&_47)

In order to allow approximately 1,500 [politically well-connected British Army officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_background_of_officers_and_other_ranks_in_the_British_Army,_1750%E2%80%931815 "Social background of officers and other ranks in the British Army, 1750–1815") to remain on active duty in America with full pay after the war, the [Bute ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bute_ministry "Bute ministry") decided in early 1763 to permanently garrison 10,000 soldiers in North America.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200555-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200555)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201651-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201651) Due to long-established [English resistance to a standing army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_resistance_to_a_standing_army "English resistance to a standing army"), stationing these officers in Great Britain during peacetime was a politically unacceptable alternative.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-32) The excuse made for a standing army in America was defense against French troops stationed in the West Indies,[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-33) and foreign populations in newly acquired territories (the French in [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Canada_(1763%E2%80%931867) "History of Canada (1763–1867)") and the Spanish in [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Florida "British Florida")). In addition, British soldiers were supposed to help collect customs duties and prevent colonists from instigating conflict with Native Americans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200555–56-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200555–56)

Instead, increased British migration beyond the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains "Appalachian Mountains") and Native American anger over the policies of General [Jeffery Amherst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffery_Amherst "Jeffery Amherst") caused [Pontiac's War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontiac's_War "Pontiac's War") (1763–1766).[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-35) In response, the [Grenville ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grenville_ministry "Grenville ministry") issued the [Royal Proclamation of 1763](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763 "Royal Proclamation of 1763"), designating the territory between the Appalachian Mountains and the Mississippi River an [Indian Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Reserve_(1763) "Indian Reserve (1763)") closed to colonial settlement. The Proclamation angered settlers, fur traders, and land speculators; however, it failed to stop westward migration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200560-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200560)

### 1764–1766: Sugar Act and Stamp Act Crisis leads to organized resistance

Main articles: [Sugar Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Act "Sugar Act"), [Currency Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Currency_Act "Currency Act"), [Quartering Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartering_Acts "Quartering Acts"), [Stamp Act 1765](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_Act_1765 "Stamp Act 1765"), and [Declaratory Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaratory_Act "Declaratory Act")

Further information: [No taxation without representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_taxation_without_representation "No taxation without representation") and [Virtual representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtual_representation "Virtual representation")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Parliament_Stamp_Act1765.jpg/250px-Parliament_Stamp_Act1765.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Parliament_Stamp_Act1765.jpg) Notice of the [Stamp Act 1765](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_Act_1765 "Stamp Act 1765") in a [colonial newspaper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_American_publishers_and_printers "Early American publishers and printers")

According to historian Middlekauff, "the need for money played a part in every important decision made by Prime Minister [George Grenville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Grenville "George Grenville") regarding the colonies—and for that matter by the ministries that followed up to 1776."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200560–61-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200560–61) The national debt had grown to £133 million with annual debt payments of £5 million (out of an £8 million annual budget). Stationing troops in North America permanently would cost another £360,000 a year. On a [per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita "Per capita") basis, Grenville claimed Americans paid 1 [shilling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shilling_(British_coin) "Shilling (British coin)") in taxes directly to the empire (not including local taxes) compared to 26 shillings paid by the English.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETaylor201651-1)[\[31\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETaylor201651) The enslaved, a near majority of the population in some colonies, did not pay any taxes and therefore skewed this comparison.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

On March 9, 1764, Grenville proposed the [Sugar Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Act "Sugar Act"), which Parliament enacted in April. It lowered the duty on foreign molasses from 6 pence to 3 pence per gallon. The [Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HM_Treasury "HM Treasury") believed it would generate £78,000. To ensure this money could be collected, the act strengthened efforts against smuggling. Customs officials could choose whether to prosecute violators in colonial courts or [vice admiralty courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_admiralty_court "Vice admiralty court"). Unlike colonial courts, where there was a [presumption of innocence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumption_of_innocence "Presumption of innocence") and local juries often acquitted accused smugglers, vice admiralty courts operated on a [presumption of guilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumption_of_guilt "Presumption of guilt"), and Crown-appointed judges decided cases.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200562,_65_&_74-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200562,_65_&_74)

Most resistance to the Sugar Act came from colonial merchants and legislators, whose primary argument was its negative economic impact on both the colonies and Great Britain. The New England and middle colonies imported molasses from the [French West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_West_Indies "French West Indies") and turned it into rum that was then [exchanged for enslaved Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade "Atlantic slave trade") sold to the southern colonies and the West Indies. This trade generated credit and [bills of exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bills_of_exchange "Bills of exchange") that the colonists used to pay for British manufactured goods. By the winter of 1764, nine colonial legislatures had petitioned the British government to repeal the Sugar Act.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200571–73-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200571–73)

Grenville decided to levy a [stamp duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_duty "Stamp duty") on the colonies as well. In February 1765, a group of colonial agents, including [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin"), met with Grenville to dissuade him. They argued that if Parliament taxed them, governors would never need to convene colonial assemblies again, thereby destroying representative government in America, but Grenville dismissed these concerns. The colonists had allies in the House of Commons, such as Colonel [Isaac Barré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Barr%C3%A9 "Isaac Barré"), who gave an impassioned speech during the debate. Still, Parliament passed the [Stamp Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_Act_1765 "Stamp Act 1765") easily, and it received [royal assent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_assent "Royal assent") in March.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200577–79-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200577–79) The act imposed [direct taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_taxes "Direct taxes") on the colonies for the first time. All official documents, newspapers, almanacs, and pamphlets were required to have the stamps—even decks of playing cards.

The colonists did not object that the taxes were high; they were actually low.[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-42)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Miller89-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-Miller89) They objected to their lack of representation in the Parliament, which gave them no voice concerning legislation that affected them, such as the tax, thereby violating [the unwritten English constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_Kingdom "Constitution of the United Kingdom"). This grievance was overly simplified with the slogan "No taxation without representation". Shortly following adoption of the Stamp Act, factions of the [Sons of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty "Sons of Liberty") formed in each colony, and began using public demonstrations, boycotts, and threats of violence to ensure that the offending British policies became unenforceable. In Boston, the Sons of Liberty burned the records of the vice admiralty court and looted the home of chief justice [Thomas Hutchinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hutchinson_(governor) "Thomas Hutchinson (governor)"). Several legislatures called for united action, and nine colonies sent delegates to the [Stamp Act Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_Act_Congress "Stamp Act Congress") in New York City in October. Moderates led by [John Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dickinson_(delegate) "John Dickinson (delegate)") drew up a [Declaration of Rights and Grievances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Rights_and_Grievances "Declaration of Rights and Grievances") stating in part that the colonists were equal to all other [British subjects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_subjects "British subjects") and that, among other abuses, taxes passed without representation in Parliament violated their [rights as Englishmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_of_Englishmen "Rights of Englishmen").

The Parliament at Westminster saw itself as the supreme lawmaking authority throughout [the Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_British_Empire "First British Empire") and thus entitled to enact any legislation, including levying any tax, without colonial approval or even consultation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-43) They argued that the colonies were legally [British corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chartered_company "Chartered company") subordinate to the British Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lecky,_William_Edward_Hartpole_1882_pp._297–98-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-Lecky,_William_Edward_Hartpole_1882_pp._297–98) Parliament insisted that the colonists effectively enjoyed a " [virtual representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtual_representation "Virtual representation")", as most British people did, since only a small minority of the British population were eligible to elect representatives to Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-45) However, Americans such as [James Otis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Otis,_Jr. "James Otis, Jr.") maintained that there was no one in Parliament responsible specifically to any colonial constituency, so they were not "virtually represented" by anyone in Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-46)

The [Rockingham government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Rockingham_ministry "First Rockingham ministry") came to power in July 1765, and Parliament debated whether to repeal the stamp tax or to send an army to enforce it. Benjamin Franklin appeared before them to make the case for repeal, explaining that the colonies had spent heavily in manpower, money, and blood defending the empire, and that further taxes to pay for those wars were unjust and might bring about a rebellion. Parliament agreed and repealed the tax on February 21, 1766, but they insisted in the [Declaratory Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaratory_Act "Declaratory Act") of March 1766 that they retained full power to make laws for the colonies "in all cases whatsoever".[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-47)[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-48) The repeal nonetheless caused widespread celebrations in the colonies.

### 1767–1773: Townshend Acts, Tea Act and the escalation of violence

Main articles: [Townshend Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Townshend_Acts "Townshend Acts") and [Tea Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Act "Tea Act")

Further information: [Crisis of 1772](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_of_1772 "Crisis of 1772"), [Massachusetts Circular Letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Circular_Letter "Massachusetts Circular Letter"), [Boston Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Massacre "Boston Massacre"), and [Boston Tea Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Tea_Party "Boston Tea Party")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Dickinson%27s_Letter_III_in_The_Pennsylvania_Chronicle.jpg/250px-Dickinson%27s_Letter_III_in_The_Pennsylvania_Chronicle.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dickinson's_Letter_III_in_The_Pennsylvania_Chronicle.jpg) Letter III of [John Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dickinson "John Dickinson")'s _[Letters from a Farmer in Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_from_a_Farmer_in_Pennsylvania "Letters from a Farmer in Pennsylvania")_, published in the _[Pennsylvania Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Chronicle "Pennsylvania Chronicle")_, December 1767

In 1767, the British Parliament passed the [Townshend Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Townshend_Acts "Townshend Acts"), which placed duties on several staple goods, including paper, glass, and tea, and established a [Board of Customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HM_Customs "HM Customs") in Boston to more rigorously execute trade regulations. The new taxes were enacted on the belief that Americans only objected to internal taxes and not to external taxes such as custom duties. [John Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dickinson "John Dickinson") however, in his widely circulated pamphlet _[Letters from a Farmer in Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_from_a_Farmer_in_Pennsylvania "Letters from a Farmer in Pennsylvania")_, argued against the constitutionality of the acts because their purpose was to raise revenue and not to regulate trade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-49) Colonists responded to the taxes by organizing new boycotts of British goods. These boycotts were less effective, however, as the goods taxed by the Townshend Acts were widely used.

In February 1768, the Assembly of [Massachusetts Bay Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Bay_Colony "Massachusetts Bay Colony") issued [a circular letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Circular_Letter "Massachusetts Circular Letter") to the other colonies urging them to coordinate resistance. The governor dissolved the assembly when it refused to rescind the letter. In June 1768 a riot broke out in Boston over the seizure of the sloop _Liberty_, owned by [John Hancock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hancock "John Hancock"), for alleged smuggling. Customs officials were forced to flee, prompting the British to deploy troops to Boston. A Boston town meeting declared that no obedience was due to parliamentary laws and called for the convening of a convention. A convention assembled but only issued a mild protest before dissolving itself. In January 1769, Parliament responded to the unrest by reactivating the [Treason Act 1543](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treason_Act_1543 "Treason Act 1543") which called for subjects outside the realm to face trials for treason in England. The governor of Massachusetts was instructed to collect evidence of said treason, and the threat caused widespread outrage, though it was not carried out.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

On March 5, 1770, a large crowd gathered around a group of British soldiers on a Boston street. The crowd grew threatening, throwing snowballs, rocks, and debris at them. One soldier was clubbed and fell.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hiller_B._Zobel_1996-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-Hiller_B._Zobel_1996) There was no order to fire, but the soldiers panicked and fired into the crowd. They hit 11 people; three civilians died of wounds at the scene of the shooting, and two died shortly after. The event quickly came to be called the [Boston Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Massacre "Boston Massacre"). The soldiers were tried and acquitted (defended by American patriot [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams "John Adams")), but the widespread descriptions soon began to turn colonial sentiment against the British. This accelerated the downward spiral in the relationship between Britain and the province of Massachusetts, and other colonies were becoming increasingly empathetic.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hiller_B._Zobel_1996-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-Hiller_B._Zobel_1996)

A new ministry under [Lord North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_North "Lord North") came to power in 1770,[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-51) and Parliament repealed most of the Townshend duties, except the tax on tea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-52) This temporarily resolved the crisis, and the boycott of British goods largely ceased, with only the more radical patriots such as [Samuel Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Adams "Samuel Adams") continuing to agitate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-53)[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-54)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/Destruction_of_the_schooner_Gasp%C3%A9_in_the_waters_of_Rhode_Island_1772_%28NYPL_b12349146-422875%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Destruction_of_the_schooner_Gasp%C3%A9_in_the_waters_of_Rhode_Island_1772_%28NYPL_b12349146-422875%29_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Destruction_of_the_schooner_Gasp%C3%A9_in_the_waters_of_Rhode_Island_1772_(NYPL_b12349146-422875)_(cropped).jpg) On June 9, 1772, the [Sons of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty "Sons of Liberty") [burned HMS _Gaspee_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaspee_Affair "Gaspee Affair"), a British customs schooner in [Narragansett Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narragansett_Bay "Narragansett Bay").

In June 1772, American patriots, including [John Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Brown_(Rhode_Island_politician) "John Brown (Rhode Island politician)"), burned a British warship that had been vigorously enforcing unpopular trade regulations, in what became known as the [_Gaspee_ Affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaspee_Affair "Gaspee Affair"). The affair was investigated for possible treason, but no action was taken.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In 1773, [private letters were published](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutchinson_letters_affair "Hutchinson letters affair") in which Massachusetts Governor Thomas Hutchinson claimed that the colonists could not enjoy all English liberties, and in which Lieutenant Governor [Andrew Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Oliver "Andrew Oliver") called for the direct payment of colonial officials, which had been paid by local authorities. This would have reduced the influence of colonial representatives over their government. The letters' contents were used as evidence of a systematic plot against American rights, and discredited Hutchinson in the eyes of the people; the colonial Assembly petitioned for his recall. Benjamin Franklin, [postmaster general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmaster_general "Postmaster general") for the colonies, acknowledged that he leaked the letters, which led to him being removed from his position.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

From Boston Samuel Adams set about creating new [Committees of Correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committees_of_Correspondence "Committees of Correspondence"), built largely on the foundations of the underground Sons of Liberty network, linking Patriots in all 13 colonies and eventually providing the framework for a [provisional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_government "Provisional government"). Virginia, the largest colony, set up its Committee of Correspondence in early 1773, on which Patrick Henry and Thomas Jefferson served.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-55) A total of about 7,000 to 8,000 Patriots served on these Committees; Loyalists were excluded. The committees became the leaders of the American resistance to British actions, and later largely determined the war effort at the state and local level. When the [First Continental Congress resolved to boycott British products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Association "Continental Association"), the colonial and local Committees took charge, examining merchant records and publishing the names of merchants who attempted to defy the boycott by importing British goods.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mary_Beth_Norton_2001_pp_144-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-Mary_Beth_Norton_2001_pp_144)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Boston_Tea_Party_w.jpg/250px-Boston_Tea_Party_w.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boston_Tea_Party_w.jpg) The December 16, 1773 [Boston Tea Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Tea_Party "Boston Tea Party"), led by [Samuel Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Adams "Samuel Adams") and [Sons of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty "Sons of Liberty"), has become a mainstay of American patriotic lore.

Meanwhile, Parliament passed the [Tea Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Act "Tea Act") lowering the price of taxed tea exported to the colonies, to help the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company "East India Company") undersell smuggled untaxed Dutch tea. Special consignees were appointed to sell the tea to bypass colonial merchants. The act was opposed by those who resisted the taxes and also by smugglers who stood to lose business.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-57)[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-58) In every colony demonstrators warned merchants not to bring in tea that included the hated new tax. In most instances, the consignees were forced by the Americans to resign and the tea was turned back, but Massachusetts governor Hutchinson refused to allow Boston merchants to give in to pressure.

A town meeting in Boston determined that the tea would not be landed, and ignored a demand from the governor to disperse. On December 16, 1773, a group of men, led by Samuel Adams and dressed to evoke the appearance of Indigenous people, boarded the ships of the East India Company and dumped £10,000 worth of tea from their holds (approximately £636,000 in 2008) into [Boston Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Harbor "Boston Harbor"). Decades later, this event became known as the [Boston Tea Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Tea_Party "Boston Tea Party") and remains a significant part of American patriotic lore.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Carp_2010_p.-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-Carp_2010_p.)

### 1774–1775: Intolerable Acts and the unifying First Continental Congress

Main articles: [Intolerable Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intolerable_Acts "Intolerable Acts") and [First Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Continental_Congress "First Continental Congress")

Further information: [Quebec Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Act "Quebec Act") and [Continental Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Association "Continental Association")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/The_able_doctor%2C_or_America_swallowing_the_bitter_draught_%28NYPL_Hades-248165-425086%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-The_able_doctor%2C_or_America_swallowing_the_bitter_draught_%28NYPL_Hades-248165-425086%29_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_able_doctor,_or_America_swallowing_the_bitter_draught_(NYPL_Hades-248165-425086)_(cropped).jpg) A 1774 illustration from _[The London Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_London_Magazine "The London Magazine")_ depicts [Prime Minister Lord North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_North,_Lord_North "Frederick North, Lord North"), author of the [Boston Port Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Port_Act "Boston Port Act"), pouring tea from a teapot labelled " [Intolerable Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intolerable_Acts "Intolerable Acts")" down the throat of [America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personification_of_the_Americas "Personification of the Americas"), whose arms are restrained by [Lord Chief Justice Mansfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Murray,_1st_Earl_of_Mansfield "William Murray, 1st Earl of Mansfield") with a tattered "Boston Petition" trampled on the ground beside her. [Lord Sandwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Montagu,_4th_Earl_of_Sandwich "John Montagu, 4th Earl of Sandwich") pins down her feet and peers up her robes; behind them, Mother [Britannia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britannia "Britannia") turns away and weeps while [personifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_personification "National personification") of France and Spain look on with smug expressions.

The British government responded by passing four laws that came to be known as the [Intolerable Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intolerable_Acts "Intolerable Acts"), further darkening colonial opinion towards England.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-60) The first was the [Massachusetts Government Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Government_Act "Massachusetts Government Act") which altered the Massachusetts charter and restricted town meetings. The second was the [Administration of Justice Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administration_of_Justice_Act_1774 "Administration of Justice Act 1774") which ordered that all British soldiers to be tried were to be arraigned in Britain, not in the colonies. The third was the [Boston Port Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Port_Act "Boston Port Act"), which closed the [port of Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Boston "Port of Boston") until the British had been compensated for the tea lost in the Boston Tea Party. The fourth was the [Quartering Act of 1774](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartering_Acts "Quartering Acts"), which allowed royal governors to house British troops in the homes of citizens without permission of the owner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-61)

In response, "Starting in August 1774, each time a court was slated to meet in some Massachusetts town, great numbers of angry citizens made sure it did not".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Raphael2002-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-Raphael2002) The next month Patriots issued the [Suffolk Resolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffolk_Resolves "Suffolk Resolves") and formed an alternative [shadow cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_cabinet "Shadow cabinet") known as the [Massachusetts Provincial Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Provincial_Congress "Massachusetts Provincial Congress"), which began training militia outside British-occupied Boston.[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-63) In September 1774, the [First Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Continental_Congress "First Continental Congress") convened in Pennsylvania, consisting of representatives from each colony, to serve as a vehicle for deliberation and collective action. During secret debates, conservative [Joseph Galloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Galloway "Joseph Galloway") proposed the [creation of a colonial Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galloway's_Plan_of_Union "Galloway's Plan of Union") that would be able to approve or disapprove acts of the British Parliament, but his idea was tabled in a vote of 6 to 5 and was subsequently removed from the record. [Congress called for a boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Association "Continental Association") beginning on December 1, 1774, of all British goods; it was enforced by [new local committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_safety_(American_Revolution) "Committee of safety (American Revolution)") authorized by the Congress that were largely built upon the earlier Committees of Correspondence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-64) It also began coordinating Patriot resistance by militias in each colony, including many veterans who had gained military experience in the French and Indian War. For the first time, the Patriots were armed and unified against Parliament.

## Military hostilities begin

Main article: [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War")

Further information: [Shot heard round the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shot_heard_round_the_world "Shot heard round the world"), [Boston campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_campaign "Boston campaign"), and [Invasion of Quebec (1775)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Quebec_(1775) "Invasion of Quebec (1775)")

King [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III") declared Massachusetts to be in a state of rebellion in February 1775[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-65) and the British garrison received orders to seize the rebels' weapons and arrest their leaders, resulting in the [Battles of Lexington and Concord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_Lexington_and_Concord "Battles of Lexington and Concord") on April 19, 1775. The Patriots assembled a militia 15,000 strong and [laid siege to Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Boston "Siege of Boston"), occupied by 6500 British soldiers. The [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress "Second Continental Congress") convened in Philadelphia on June 14, 1775. The congress was divided on the best course of action. They authorized formation of the [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army "Continental Army"), appointing George Washington as its commander-in-chief, while producing the [Olive Branch Petition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_Branch_Petition "Olive Branch Petition") in which they attempted to come to an accord with King George. The king, however, would issue a [Proclamation of Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Rebellion "Proclamation of Rebellion") in August which declared that all the states were "in rebellion" and the members of Congress were traitors. Meanwhile, the [Battle of Bunker Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bunker_Hill "Battle of Bunker Hill") on June 17, 1775, was a British victory—but at a great cost: about 1,000 British casualties from a garrison of about 6,000, as compared to 500 American casualties from a much larger force.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-66)[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-67)

As [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin") wrote to [Joseph Priestley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Priestley "Joseph Priestley") in October 1775:

> Britain, at the expense of three millions, has killed 150 Yankees this campaign, which is £20,000 a head... During the same time, 60,000 children have been born in America. From these data his mathematical head will easily calculate the time and expense necessary to kill us all.[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-68)

In the winter of 1775, in the hopes of gaining the support of French-speaking colonists recently conquered by the British,[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-69) the Americans [invaded northeastern Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Quebec_(1775) "Invasion of Quebec (1775)") under generals [Benedict Arnold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_Arnold "Benedict Arnold") and [Richard Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Montgomery "Richard Montgomery").[&#8202;](#ref-back-KingsfordV391-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-KingsfordV391) The attack was a failure; many Americans were killed, captured, or died of smallpox.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lanctot126-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-Lanctot126)

## From rebellion to revolution

Following the Battle of Bunker Hill in June 1775, the Patriots had control of Massachusetts outside Boston's city limits, and the Loyalists suddenly found themselves on the defensive without the protection of the British army. In each of the Thirteen Colonies, American patriots overthrew their existing governments, closed courts, and drove out British colonial officials. They held elected conventions and established [their own legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_Congress "Provincial Congress"), which existed outside any legal parameters established by the British. The Americans drafted new constitutions to supersede royal charters, proclaiming that they were now _[states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_state "Sovereign state")_, no longer _[colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony "Colony")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pole-ch29-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-Pole-ch29)

### Creating new state constitutions

Main article: [State constitution (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_constitution_(United_States) "State constitution (United States)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Samuel_Adams_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg/250px-Samuel_Adams_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Samuel_Adams_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg) [Samuel Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Adams "Samuel Adams") points at the [Massachusetts Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explanatory_charter "Explanatory charter"), which he viewed as a constitution that protected the people's rights, in this c.1772 portrait by [John Singleton Copley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Singleton_Copley "John Singleton Copley").[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-73)

On January 5, 1776, [New Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Hampshire "Province of New Hampshire") ratified the first state constitution. In May 1776, Congress voted to suppress all forms of Crown authority, to be replaced by locally created authority. [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey "Province of New Jersey"), [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_South_Carolina "Province of South Carolina"), and [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia "Colony of Virginia") created their constitutions before July 4. [Rhode Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Rhode_Island_and_Providence_Plantations "Colony of Rhode Island and Providence Plantations") and [Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Colony "Connecticut Colony") simply took their existing [royal charters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_charter "Royal charter") and deleted all references to the Crown.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-74) The new states were all committed to republicanism, with no inherited offices. On May 26, 1776, John Adams wrote [James Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Sullivan_(governor) "James Sullivan (governor)") from Philadelphia, warning against extending [the franchise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_franchise "The franchise") too far:

> Depend upon it, sir, it is dangerous to open so fruitful a source of controversy and altercation, as would be opened by attempting to alter the qualifications of voters. There will be no end of it. New claims will arise. Women will demand a vote. Lads from twelve to twenty one will think their rights not enough attended to, and every man, who has not a farthing, will demand an equal voice with any other in all acts of state. It tends to confound and destroy all distinctions, and prostrate all ranks, to one common level\[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[.\][\[74\]](#refnote-75)[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-76)

The resulting constitutions in states, including those of [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Delaware "Constitution of Delaware"), [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Maryland "Constitution of Maryland"), [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Massachusetts "Constitution of Massachusetts"), [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_York "Constitution of New York"), and [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Virginia "Constitution of Virginia")[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-77) featured:

- Property qualifications for voting and even more substantial requirements for elected positions (though New York and Maryland lowered property qualifications)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pole-ch29-1)[\[71\]](#refnote-Pole-ch29)
- [Bicameral legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral_legislature "Bicameral legislature"), with the upper house as a check on the lower
- Strong [governors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor "Governor") with veto power over the legislature and substantial appointment authority
- Few or no restraints on individuals holding multiple positions in government
- The continuation of [state-established religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Established_church "Established church")

In [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Pennsylvania "Constitution of Pennsylvania"), [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Jersey "Constitution of New Jersey"), and [New Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Hampshire "Constitution of New Hampshire"), the resulting constitutions embodied:

- universal manhood suffrage, or minimal property requirements for voting or holding office (New Jersey enfranchised some property-owning widows, a step that it retracted 25 years later)
- strong, [unicameral legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicameralism "Unicameralism")
- relatively weak governors without veto powers, and with little appointing authority
- prohibition against individuals holding multiple government posts

The radical provisions of [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania "Pennsylvania")'s constitution lasted 14 years. In 1790, conservatives gained power in the state legislature, called a new constitutional convention, and rewrote the constitution. The new constitution substantially reduced universal male suffrage, gave the governor veto power and patronage appointment authority, and added an upper house with substantial wealth qualifications to the unicameral legislature. [Thomas Paine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Paine "Thomas Paine") called it a constitution unworthy of America.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wood,_1992-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-Wood,_1992)

### Declaration of Independence

Main articles: [Committee of Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Five "Committee of Five"), [Lee Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Resolution "Lee Resolution"), and [United States Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence")

In March 1776, aided by the [fortification of Dorchester Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortification_of_Dorchester_Heights "Fortification of Dorchester Heights") with cannons recently [captured at Fort Ticonderoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Fort_Ticonderoga "Capture of Fort Ticonderoga"), the Continental Army led by George Washington forced the British to [evacuate Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation_Day_(Massachusetts) "Evacuation Day (Massachusetts)"). The revolutionaries now fully controlled all thirteen states and ready to declare joint independence. There still were many Loyalists, but they were no longer in control anywhere by July 1776, and all of the royal officials had fled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-79)

In April 1776, the [North Carolina Provincial Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Provincial_Congress "North Carolina Provincial Congress") issued the [Halifax Resolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halifax_Resolves "Halifax Resolves") explicitly authorizing its delegates to vote for independence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-80) By June, nine state legislatures supported independence from Britain, and Pennsylvania, Delaware, Maryland, and New York followed. [Richard Henry Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Henry_Lee "Richard Henry Lee") was instructed by the Virginia legislature to propose independence, and he did so on June 7, 1776.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/William_Walcutt_statue_George_III.png/500px-William_Walcutt_statue_George_III.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:William_Walcutt_statue_George_III.png) _Pulling Down the Statue of King George III, N.Y.C._, depicting American patriots tearing down a statue of King [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III") in New York City on July 9, 1776, immediately after Commander-in-Chief George Washington had the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence") read aloud to the Continental Army and the public on the [city's common green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Hall_Park "City Hall Park") five days after it was issued in Philadelphia.

Gathered at Pennsylvania State House in Philadelphia, later renamed [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall "Independence Hall"), 56 of the nation's [Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States "Founding Fathers of the United States") representing the [United Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Colonies "United Colonies") adopted and issued to King [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III") the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence"). It had been drafted largely by [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson "Thomas Jefferson"), and was edited and presented by the [Committee of Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Five "Committee of Five") which was charged with authoring it. The Congress struck several provisions of the committee's draft, and then adopted it unanimously on July 4.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-81) The Declaration embodied [universal rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law") expressed through the political philosophies of [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States "Liberalism in the United States") and [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States "Republicanism in the United States"), rejected [monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy "Monarchy") and [aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristocracy "Aristocracy"), and famously proclaimed that " [all men are created equal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_men_are_created_equal "All men are created equal")". What had begun as a rebellion demanding the rights of Englishmen had now evolved into a revolution to overthrow and replace the political and social order in America based on universal rights.

### "Perpetual Union"

Main article: [Articles of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation "Articles of Confederation")

With the new state constitutions and the issuance of the Declaration of Independence, each former colony began operating as an independent and sovereign state. The next step was to form a [union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_union "Political union") to facilitate international relations and alliances.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-82)[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-83)

On November 5, 1777, the Congress approved the [Articles of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation "Articles of Confederation") and Perpetual Union and sent it to each state for ratification. The Congress immediately began operating under the Articles' terms, providing a structure of [shared sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation "Confederation") during prosecution of the Revolutionary War. The Articles were fully ratified on March 1, 1781. At that point, the Continental Congress was dissolved and a new government of the [United States in Congress Assembled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_Confederation "Congress of the Confederation") took its place the following day, with [Samuel Huntington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Huntington_(statesman) "Samuel Huntington (statesman)") leading the Congress as presiding officer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-84)[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-85) It remained a [unicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicameral "Unicameral") body, where each state retained one vote.

## Defending the revolution

Main article: [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War")

### British return: 1776–1777

Further information: [Northern theater of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Northern theater of the American Revolutionary War"), [New York and New Jersey campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_and_New_Jersey_campaign "New York and New Jersey campaign"), [Staten Island Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staten_Island_Peace_Conference "Staten Island Peace Conference"), [Saratoga campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saratoga_campaign "Saratoga campaign"), and [Philadelphia campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign "Philadelphia campaign")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/The_British_fleet_in_the_lower_bay_1876.jpg/250px-The_British_fleet_in_the_lower_bay_1876.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_British_fleet_in_the_lower_bay_1876.jpg) The British fleet amassed off [Staten Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staten_Island "Staten Island") in [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor "New York Harbor") in the summer of 1776, as depicted in _[Harper's Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harper's_Magazine "Harper's Magazine")_ in 1876

According to British historian [Jeremy Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Black_(historian) "Jeremy Black (historian)"), the British had significant advantages, including a highly trained army, the world's largest navy, and an efficient system of public finance that could easily fund the war. However, they seriously misunderstood the depth of support for the American Patriot position, misinterpreting the situation as merely a large-scale riot. The British government believed that they could overawe the Americans by sending a large military and naval force:

> "Convinced that the Revolution was the work of a full few miscreants who had rallied an armed rabble to their cause, they expected that the revolutionaries would be intimidated.... Then the vast majority of Americans, who were loyal but cowed by the terroristic tactics... would rise up, kick out the rebels, and restore loyal government in each colony."[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-86)

In the [Siege of Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Boston "Siege of Boston"), Washington forced the British out of the city in the spring of 1776, and neither the British nor the Loyalists controlled any significant areas. The British, however, were amassing forces at their naval base at [Halifax, Nova Scotia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halifax_Regional_Municipality "Halifax Regional Municipality"). They returned in force in July 1776, landing in New York and defeating Washington's Continental Army toe-to-toe in August at the [Battle of Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Long_Island "Battle of Long Island"), the largest battle of the war. Following that victory, and poised to take New York City, they requested a meeting with representatives from Congress to negotiate an end to hostilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Schecter,_Barnet_2002-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-Schecter,_Barnet_2002)[&#8202;](#ref-back-McCullough,_1776_2005-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-McCullough,_1776_2005)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Us_unabhaengigkeitskrieg.jpg/250px-Us_unabhaengigkeitskrieg.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Us_unabhaengigkeitskrieg.jpg) [Hessian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hessian_(soldiers) "Hessian (soldiers)") troops hired out to the British by their German sovereigns

A delegation including John Adams and Benjamin Franklin met British admiral [Richard Howe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Howe,_1st_Earl_Howe "Richard Howe, 1st Earl Howe") on [Staten Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staten_Island "Staten Island") in [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor "New York Harbor") on September 11 in what became known as the [Staten Island Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staten_Island_Peace_Conference "Staten Island Peace Conference"). Howe demanded that the Americans retract the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence"), which they refused to do, and negotiations ended. The British then [seized New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kip's_Bay "Battle of Kip's Bay") and nearly captured Washington's army.
