They made the city their main political and military base of operations, holding the strategic harbor and port until [November 1783](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation_Day_(New_York) "Evacuation Day (New York)"). The city became the destination for Loyalist refugees and a focal point of Washington's [intelligence network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Intelligence in the American Revolutionary War").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Schecter,_Barnet_2002-1)[\[85\]](#refnote-Schecter,_Barnet_2002)[&#8202;](#ref-back-McCullough,_1776_2005-1)[\[86\]](#refnote-McCullough,_1776_2005)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_by_Emanuel_Leutze%2C_MMA-NYC%2C_1851.jpg/250px-Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_by_Emanuel_Leutze%2C_MMA-NYC%2C_1851.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_by_Emanuel_Leutze,_MMA-NYC,_1851.jpg) [Washington crossing the Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington's_crossing_of_the_Delaware_River "George Washington's crossing of the Delaware River") on December 25–26, 1776, depicted in [Emanuel Leutze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_Leutze "Emanuel Leutze")'s [1851 painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_(1851_paintings) "Washington Crossing the Delaware (1851 paintings)")

The British also took New Jersey, pushing the Continental Army into Pennsylvania. Washington [crossed the Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington's_crossing_of_the_Delaware_River "George Washington's crossing of the Delaware River") back into New Jersey in a surprise attack in late December 1776 and defeated the [Hessian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hessian_(soldier) "Hessian (soldier)") and British [garrisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garrisons "Garrisons") at [Trenton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Trenton "Battle of Trenton") and [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Princeton "Battle of Princeton"), thereby regaining control of most of New Jersey. The victories gave an important boost to Patriots at a time when morale was flagging and [enlistments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlistment "Enlistment") were ending, and they have become iconic events of the war.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In 1777, the British sent [Burgoyne's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Burgoyne "John Burgoyne") invasion force from Canada south through New York with the aim of isolating New England to the east, which the British perceived as the primary source of agitation. In anticipation of a [coordinated British attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign "Philadelphia campaign") on the _de facto_ revolutionary capital of Philadelphia, the Continental Congress was forced to temporarily depart Philadelphia in September for [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore "Baltimore"), where they continued deliberations. Rather than move north to support Burgoyne, the British army in New York City in fact went to Philadelphia in a major case of mis-coordination, capturing it from Washington. The invasion army from Canada was much too slow and became trapped in northern New York state. It surrendered to the Americans after the [Battles of Saratoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_Saratoga "Battles of Saratoga") in October 1777. From early October 1777 until November 15, a siege distracted British troops at [Fort Mifflin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Mifflin "Fort Mifflin"), Pennsylvania and allowed Washington time to preserve the Continental Army by safely leading his troops to harsh winter quarters at [Valley Forge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_Forge "Valley Forge"), some 35 miles from Philadelphia.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Prisoners

Main article: [Prisoners of war in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoners_of_war_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Prisoners of war in the American Revolutionary War")

Further information: [HMS Jersey (1736)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Jersey_(1736) "HMS Jersey (1736)") and [Sugar house prisons in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_house_prisons_in_New_York_City "Sugar house prisons in New York City")

On August 23, 1775, George III declared Americans to be traitors to the Crown if they took up arms against royal authority. There were thousands of British and Hessian soldiers in American hands following their surrender at the Battles of Saratoga. Lord Germain took a hard line, but the British generals on American soil never held treason trials, and instead treated captured American soldiers as prisoners of war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-89) The dilemma was that tens of thousands of Loyalists were under American control and American retaliation would have been easy. The British built much of their strategy around using these Loyalists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Larry_G._Bowman_1976-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-Larry_G._Bowman_1976) The British maltreated the prisoners whom they held, resulting in more American deaths in the prisons than all the American battlefield mortalities, combined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Larry_G._Bowman_1976-1)[\[88\]](#refnote-Larry_G._Bowman_1976) At the end of the war, both sides released their surviving prisoners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-91)

### American alliances after 1778

Main article: [Diplomacy in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomacy_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Diplomacy in the American Revolutionary War")

Further information: [France in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "France in the American Revolutionary War"), [Spain in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Spain in the American Revolutionary War"), and [Carlisle Peace Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlisle_Peace_Commission "Carlisle Peace Commission")

See also: [First League of Armed Neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_League_of_Armed_Neutrality "First League of Armed Neutrality")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Conrad_Alexandre_G%C3%A9rard_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Conrad_Alexandre_G%C3%A9rard_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Conrad_Alexandre_G%C3%A9rard_portrait_(cropped).jpg) [Conrad Alexandre Gérard de Rayneval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conrad_Alexandre_G%C3%A9rard_de_Rayneval "Conrad Alexandre Gérard de Rayneval"), first French representative to the United States and signatory to the alliance treaties.

The capture of a British army at Saratoga encouraged the French to formally enter the war in support of Congress, and Benjamin Franklin negotiated a permanent military alliance in early 1778; France thus became the first foreign nation to officially recognize the Declaration of Independence. On February 6, 1778, the United States and France signed the [Treaty of Amity and Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Amity_and_Commerce_(USA%E2%80%93France) "Treaty of Amity and Commerce (USA–France)") and the [Treaty of Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alliance_(1778) "Treaty of Alliance (1778)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hamilton,_1974_p._28-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-Hamilton,_1974_p._28) [William Pitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham "William Pitt, 1st Earl of Chatham") spoke out in Parliament urging Britain to make peace with the Americans and to unite against France, while British politicians who had sympathized with colonial grievances now turned against the Americans for allying with Britain's rival and enemy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-93)

The Spanish and the Dutch became allies of the French in 1779 and 1780 respectively, forcing the British to fight a global war without major allies, and requiring it to slip through a combined blockade of the Atlantic. The Dutch Republic, also at war with Britain, was the next country after France to sign a treaty with the United States, on October 8, 1782.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hamilton,_1974_p._28-1)[\[90\]](#refnote-Hamilton,_1974_p._28) On April 3, 1783, Ambassador Extraordinary [Gustaf Philip Creutz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustaf_Philip_Creutz "Gustaf Philip Creutz"), representing King [Gustav III of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_III_of_Sweden "Gustav III of Sweden"), and Benjamin Franklin, signed a [Treaty of Amity and Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Amity_and_Commerce_(USA%E2%80%93Sweden) "Treaty of Amity and Commerce (USA–Sweden)") with the U.S.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hamilton,_1974_p._28-2)[\[90\]](#refnote-Hamilton,_1974_p._28)

### 1778–1783: the British move south

Further information: [Southern theater of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Southern theater of the American Revolutionary War") and [Naval operations in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_operations_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Naval operations in the American Revolutionary War")

Britain began to view the American Revolutionary War as merely one front in a wider war,[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-94) and the British chose to withdraw troops from America to reinforce the British colonies in the Caribbean, which were under threat of Spanish or French invasion. British commander Sir [Henry Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Clinton_(American_War_of_Independence) "Henry Clinton (American War of Independence)") evacuated Philadelphia and returned to New York City. Washington intercepted him in the [Battle of Monmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Monmouth "Battle of Monmouth") Court House, the last major battle fought in the north. After an inconclusive engagement, the British retreated to New York City. The northern war subsequently became a stalemate, as the focus of attention shifted to the smaller southern theater.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Higginbotham,_1983_p._83-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-Higginbotham,_1983_p._83)

The [British Royal Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Royal_Navy "British Royal Navy") blockaded ports and held New York City for the duration of the war, and other cities for brief periods, but failed in their effort to destroy Washington's forces. The British strategy now concentrated on a campaign in the [southern states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Colonies "Southern Colonies"). Due to a lack of regular troops at their disposal compared to the Americans,\[ _[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] the British commanders saw the "southern strategy" as a more viable plan. They perceived the Southern Colonies as strongly Loyalist, with a large population of recent British immigrants, and large numbers of slaves who might be tempted to run away from their Patriot masters to join the British and gain their freedom.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-96)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Cowpens.jpg/250px-Cowpens.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cowpens.jpg) The Battle of Cowpens, depicting [the 1781 battle in South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Cowpens "Battle of Cowpens"), painted by William Ranney in 1845.

Beginning in late December 1778, the British captured [Savannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia "Savannah, Georgia") and controlled the [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Georgia "Province of Georgia") coastline. In 1780, they launched a fresh invasion and [took Charleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Charleston "Siege of Charleston"). A significant victory at the [Battle of Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Camden "Battle of Camden") meant that royal forces soon controlled most of Georgia and South Carolina. The British set up a network of forts inland, hoping that the Loyalists would rally to the flag.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Henry_Lumpkin_2000-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-Henry_Lumpkin_2000) Not enough Loyalists turned out, however, and the British had to fight their way north into North Carolina and Virginia with a severely weakened army. Behind them, much of the territory that they had already captured dissolved into a chaotic [guerrilla war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_war "Guerrilla war"), fought predominantly between bands of Loyalists and American militia, which negated many of the gains that the British had previously made.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Henry_Lumpkin_2000-1)[\[95\]](#refnote-Henry_Lumpkin_2000)

#### Surrender at Yorktown (1781)

Main article: [Siege of Yorktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Yorktown "Siege of Yorktown")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/John_Trumbull_-_The_Surrender_of_Lord_Cornwallis_at_Yorktown%2C_October_19%2C_1781_-_1832.4_-_Yale_University_Art_Gallery.jpg/250px-John_Trumbull_-_The_Surrender_of_Lord_Cornwallis_at_Yorktown%2C_October_19%2C_1781_-_1832.4_-_Yale_University_Art_Gallery.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:John_Trumbull_-_The_Surrender_of_Lord_Cornwallis_at_Yorktown,_October_19,_1781_-_1832.4_-_Yale_University_Art_Gallery.jpg) The 1781 [siege of Yorktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Yorktown "Siege of Yorktown") ended with the surrender of a second British army, marking effective British defeat.

The British army under Cornwallis marched to [Yorktown, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorktown,_Virginia "Yorktown, Virginia"), where they expected to be rescued by a British fleet.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-98) The fleet did arrive, but so did a larger French fleet. The French were victorious in the [Battle of the Chesapeake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Chesapeake "Battle of the Chesapeake"), and the British fleet returned to New York for reinforcements, leaving Cornwallis trapped. In October 1781, the British surrendered their second invading army of the war under a siege by the combined French and Continental armies commanded by Washington.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-99)

### End of the war

Washington did not know if or when the British might reopen hostilities after Yorktown. They still had 26,000 troops occupying New York City, Charleston, and Savannah, together with a powerful fleet. The French army and navy departed, so the Americans were on their own in 1782–83.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-100) The American treasury was empty, and the unpaid soldiers were growing restive, almost to the point of mutiny or possible _[coup d'etat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_d'etat "Coup d'etat")_. Washington dispelled the unrest among officers of the [Newburgh Conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newburgh_Conspiracy "Newburgh Conspiracy") in 1783, and Congress subsequently created the promise of a five years bonus for all officers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-101)

Historians continue to debate whether the odds were long or short for American victory. [John E. Ferling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_E._Ferling "John E. Ferling") says that the odds were so long that the American victory was "almost a miracle".[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-102) On the other hand, [Joseph Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Ellis "Joseph Ellis") says that the odds favored the Americans, and asks whether there ever was any realistic chance for the British to win. He argues that this opportunity came only once, in the summer of 1776, where Admiral Howe and his brother General Howe "missed several opportunities to destroy the Continental Army.... Chance, luck, and even the vagaries of the weather played crucial roles." Ellis's point is that the strategic and tactical decisions of the Howes were fatally flawed because they underestimated the challenges posed by the Patriots. Ellis concludes that, once the Howe brothers failed, the opportunity "would never come again" for a British victory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-103)

Public support for the conflict had never been strong in Britain, where many sympathized with the Americans, but now it reached a new low.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-104) King George wanted to fight on, but his supporters lost control of Parliament and the British launched no further offensives in America on the eastern seaboard.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Higginbotham,_1983_p._83-1)[\[93\]](#refnote-Higginbotham,_1983_p._83)[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-106)

## Paris peace treaty

Main article: [Treaty of Paris (1783)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1783) "Treaty of Paris (1783)")

Further information: [Diplomacy in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomacy_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Diplomacy in the American Revolutionary War") and [Peace of Paris (1783)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Paris_(1783) "Peace of Paris (1783)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Treaty_of_Paris_by_Benjamin_West_1783.jpg/250px-Treaty_of_Paris_by_Benjamin_West_1783.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Treaty_of_Paris_by_Benjamin_West_1783.jpg) _[Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(painting) "Treaty of Paris (painting)")_ by [Benjamin West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_West "Benjamin West") portrays the American delegation about to sign the 1783 [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1783) "Treaty of Paris (1783)") ( [John Jay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Jay "John Jay"), [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams "John Adams"), [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin"), [Henry Laurens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Laurens "Henry Laurens"), [W. T. Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Temple_Franklin "William Temple Franklin")). The British delegation refused to pose and the painting was never completed.

During negotiations in Paris, the American delegation discovered that France supported American independence but no territorial gains, hoping to confine the new nation to the area east of the Appalachian Mountains. The Americans opened direct secret negotiations with London, cutting out the French. British Prime Minister [Lord Shelburne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Petty,_2nd_Earl_of_Shelburne "William Petty, 2nd Earl of Shelburne") was in charge of the British negotiations, and he saw a chance to make the United States a valuable economic partner, facilitating trade and investment opportunities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-107) The US obtained all the land east of the Mississippi River, including [southern Canada below the Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory "Northwest Territory"), but Spain took control of Florida from the British. It gained fishing rights off Canadian coasts, and agreed to allow British merchants and Loyalists to recover their property. Prime Minister Shelburne foresaw highly profitable two-way trade between Britain and the rapidly growing United States, which did come to pass. The blockade was lifted and American merchants were free to trade with any nation anywhere in the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-108)

The British largely abandoned their Indigenous allies, who were not a party to this treaty and did not recognize it until they were defeated militarily by the United States. However, the British did sell them munitions and maintained forts in American territory until the [Jay Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Treaty "Jay Treaty") of 1795.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-109)

## Finance

Main article: [Financial costs of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_costs_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Financial costs of the American Revolutionary War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States_with_Robert_Morris%2C_Jr._statue%2C_Philadelphia.jpg/250px-Second_Bank_of_the_United_States_with_Robert_Morris%2C_Jr._statue%2C_Philadelphia.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Second_Bank_of_the_United_States_with_Robert_Morris,_Jr._statue,_Philadelphia.jpg) [Robert Morris statue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Morris_(Bartlett) "Robert Morris (Bartlett)") honoring [Founding Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States "Founding Fathers of the United States") and financier [Robert Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Morris_(financier) "Robert Morris (financier)") at [Independence National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_National_Historical_Park "Independence National Historical Park") in [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia "Philadelphia")[![A five-dollar banknote issued by the Second Continental Congress in 1775.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Verso_The_United_Colonies_5_dollars_1775_urn-3_HBS.Baker.AC_1142110.jpeg/250px-Verso_The_United_Colonies_5_dollars_1775_urn-3_HBS.Baker.AC_1142110.jpeg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Verso_The_United_Colonies_5_dollars_1775_urn-3_HBS.Baker.AC_1142110.jpeg) A five dollar banknote issued by the [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress "Second Continental Congress") in 1775

Britain's war against the Americans, the French, and the Spanish cost about £100million. The Treasury borrowed 40 percent of the money that it needed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-110) Britain had a sophisticated financial system based on the wealth of thousands of landowners who supported the government, together with banks and financiers in London. In London the British had relatively little difficulty financing their war, keeping their suppliers and soldiers paid, and hiring tens of thousands of German soldiers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-John_Brewer_1990_p_91-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-John_Brewer_1990_p_91)

In sharp contrast, Congress and the American states had no end of difficulty financing the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-112) In 1775, there was at most 12million dollars in gold in the colonies, not nearly enough to cover current transactions, let alone finance a major war. The British made the situation much worse by imposing a tight blockade on every American port, which cut off almost all trade. One partial solution was to rely on volunteer support from militiamen and donations from patriotic citizens.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Charles_Rappleye_2010_pp_225-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-Charles_Rappleye_2010_pp_225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-114) Another was to delay actual payments, pay soldiers and suppliers in depreciated currency, and promise that it would be made good after the war. Indeed, the soldiers and officers were given land grants in 1783 to cover the wages that they had earned but had not been paid during the war.

Starting in 1776, the Congress sought to raise money by loans from wealthy individuals, promising to redeem the bonds after the war. The bonds were redeemed in 1791 at face value, but the scheme raised little money because Americans had little specie, and many of the richest merchants were supporters of the Crown. Beginning in 1777, Congress repeatedly asked the states to provide money, but it had no system of taxation to require funds, and the states were of little help. By 1780, Congress was making requisitions for specific supplies of corn, beef, pork, and other necessities, an inefficient system which barely kept the army alive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-115)[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-116)

Congress used four main methods to cover the cost of the war, which cost about 66million dollars in specie (gold and silver).[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-117) Significantly, the French secretly supplied the Americans with money, gunpowder, and munitions to weaken Great Britain; the subsidies continued when France entered the war in 1778, and the French government and Paris bankers lent about 18 million _[livres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_livre "French livre")_ to the American war effort.[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-118) Congress also made issues of paper money, known colloquially as " [Continental Dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_currency_banknotes "Continental currency banknotes")", in 1775–1780 and in 1780–1781. The first issue amounted to 242million dollars. This paper money would supposedly be redeemed for state taxes,\[ _[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] but the holders were eventually paid off in 1791 at the rate of one cent on the dollar\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]. By 1780, the paper money was so devalued that the phrase "not worth a Continental" became synonymous with valueless.[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-119) The skyrocketing inflation was a hardship on the few people who had fixed incomes, but 90 percent of the people were farmers and were not directly affected by it. Debtors benefited by paying off their debts with depreciated paper. The greatest burden was borne by the soldiers of the Continental Army whose wages were usually paid late and depreciated in value every month, weakening their morale and adding to the hardships of their families.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-120)

The national government did not have a strong leader in financial matters until 1781, when [Robert Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Morris_(financier) "Robert Morris (financier)") was named [Superintendent of Finance of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superintendent_of_Finance_of_the_United_States "Superintendent of Finance of the United States").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Charles_Rappleye_2010_pp_225-1)[\[110\]](#refnote-Charles_Rappleye_2010_pp_225) Morris used a French loan in 1782 to set up the private [Bank of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_North_America "Bank of North America") to finance the war. He reduced the [civil list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_list "Civil list"), saved money by using competitive bidding for contracts, tightened accounting procedures, and demanded the national government's full share of money and supplies from the individual states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Charles_Rappleye_2010_pp_225-2)[\[110\]](#refnote-Charles_Rappleye_2010_pp_225)

## "A more perfect union"

Main articles: [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution "United States Constitution") and [United States Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights "United States Bill of Rights")

See also: [Annapolis Convention (1786)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annapolis_Convention_(1786) "Annapolis Convention (1786)"), [Philadelphia Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Convention "Philadelphia Convention"), and [The Federalist Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Federalist_Papers "The Federalist Papers")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Scene_at_the_Signing_of_the_Constitution_of_the_United_States.jpg/250px-Scene_at_the_Signing_of_the_Constitution_of_the_United_States.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Scene_at_the_Signing_of_the_Constitution_of_the_United_States.jpg) The September 17, 1787 [signing of the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signing_of_the_United_States_Constitution "Signing of the United States Constitution") at [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall "Independence Hall") in [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia "Philadelphia") depicted in [Howard Chandler Christy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Chandler_Christy "Howard Chandler Christy")'s 1940 painting, _[Scene at the Signing of the Constitution of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scene_at_the_Signing_of_the_Constitution_of_the_United_States "Scene at the Signing of the Constitution of the United States")_

The war ended in 1783 and was followed by a period of slow economic recovery that accompanied the end of mercantilism and the resumption and expansion of worldwide trade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-121) The national government was still operating under the Articles of Confederation and [settled the issue of the western territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Ordinance "Northwest Ordinance"), which the states ceded to Congress. American settlers moved rapidly into those areas, with Vermont, Kentucky, and Tennessee becoming states in the 1790s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-122)

However, the national government had no authority to tax, no money either to pay the war debts owed to European nations and the private banks, or to pay Americans who had been given millions of dollars of promissory notes for supplies during the war. Nationalists led by Washington, Alexander Hamilton, and other veterans feared that the new nation was too fragile to withstand an international war, or even the repetition of internal revolts such as the [Shays's Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shays's_Rebellion "Shays's Rebellion") of 1786 in Massachusetts. They convinced the Congress of the Confederation to call the [Philadelphia Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Convention "Philadelphia Convention") in 1787.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-123) The Convention adopted a new [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States "Constitution of the United States") which provided for a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic "Republic") with authority to tax and a much stronger national government in a [federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism "Federalism") framework, including an effective executive in a [check-and-balance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers "Separation of powers") system with the judiciary and legislature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-124) The Constitution was ratified in 1788, after a fierce debate in the states over the proposed new government. The [new administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_Washington "Presidency of George Washington") under President George Washington took office in New York in March 1789 and the new bicameral [Congress of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_United_States "Congress of the United States") was seated.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-125) [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison "James Madison") spearheaded Congressional legislation [proposing amendments to the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Five_of_the_United_States_Constitution "Article Five of the United States Constitution") as assurances to those cautious about federal power, guaranteeing many of the [inalienable rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law") that formed a foundation for the revolution. Rhode Island was the final state to ratify the Constitution in 1790, the first ten amendments were ratified in 1791 and became known as the [United States Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights "United States Bill of Rights").

### National debt

Further information: [Financial costs of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_costs_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Financial costs of the American Revolutionary War"), [National debt of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_debt_of_the_United_States "National debt of the United States"), and [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton "Alexander Hamilton")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/HAMILTON%2C_Alexander-Treasury_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-HAMILTON%2C_Alexander-Treasury_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:HAMILTON,_Alexander-Treasury_(BEP_engraved_portrait)_(cropped).jpg) [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton "Alexander Hamilton"), the first [Secretary of the Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury "United States Secretary of the Treasury") during the [Presidency of George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_Washington "Presidency of George Washington")

The national debt fell into three categories after the Revolutionary War. The first was the $12million owed to foreigners, mostly money borrowed from France. There was general agreement to pay the foreign debts at full value. The national government owed $40million and state governments owed $25million to Americans who had sold food, horses, and supplies to the Patriot forces. There were also other debts which consisted of [promissory notes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promissory_note "Promissory note") issued during the war to soldiers, merchants, and farmers who accepted these payments on the premise that the new nation would create a government that would pay these debts eventually.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The Americans struggled to pay off the French loans; they ceased making interest payments to France in 1785 and defaulted on installments due in 1787. In 1790, however, they resumed regular payments on their debts to the French.[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-126) The war expenses of the individual states added up to $114million, compared to $37million by the central government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-127) In 1790, Congress combined the remaining state debts with the foreign and domestic debts into one national debt totaling $80million at the recommendation of first Secretary of the Treasury [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton "Alexander Hamilton"). Everyone received face value for wartime certificates, so that the national honor would be sustained and the national credit established.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-128)

Accounts with the French government were settled in 1795 when [James Swan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Swan_(financier) "James Swan (financier)"), an American banker acting as agent for France, helped refinance the United States' remaining debt to France. The American government issued bonds in the amount of the remaining debt and sold them publicly, with the proceeds going to France through Swan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-129) The United States no longer owed money to foreign governments, although it continued to owe money to private investors both in the United States and in Europe.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Historian2009-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-Historian2009) This allowed the young United States to place itself on a sound financial footing.

## Ideology and factions

The population of the Thirteen States was not homogeneous in political views and attitudes. Loyalties and allegiances varied widely within regions and communities and even within families, and sometimes shifted during the Revolution.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Ideology behind the revolution

Main articles: [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment "Age of Enlightenment") and [American Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Enlightenment "American Enlightenment")

The American Enlightenment was a critical precursor of the American Revolution. Chief among the ideas of the American Enlightenment were the concepts of [natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law"), natural rights, consent of the governed, [self-determination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-determination "Self-determination"), [equality under the law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_before_the_law "Equality before the law"),
liberalism, republicanism, and intolerance of political corruption. A growing number of American colonists embraced these views and fostered an intellectual environment which led to a new sense of political and social identity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-131)

#### Liberalism

Main article: [Liberalism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States "Liberalism in the United States")

See also: [Social contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_contract "Social contract") and [Natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/LockeTreatiseAmerica.png/250px-LockeTreatiseAmerica.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LockeTreatiseAmerica.png) The only edition of Locke's _Treatises_ published in colonial America during the 18th century (1773)

[John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke "John Locke") is often referred to as "the philosopher of the American Revolution" due to his work in the [Social Contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Contract "Social Contract") and [Natural Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law") theories that underpinned the Revolution's political ideology.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-132) Locke's _[Two Treatises of Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Treatises_of_Government "Two Treatises of Government")_ published in 1689 was especially influential. He argued that all humans were created equally free, and governments therefore needed the " [consent of the governed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed "Consent of the governed")". This contrasted and conflicted sharply with the British system of inherited political power and hierarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-133) In late eighteenth-century America, belief was still widespread in "equality by creation" and "rights by creation".[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-134) Locke's ideas on liberty influenced the political thinking of English writers such as [John Trenchard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Trenchard_(writer) "John Trenchard (writer)"), [Thomas Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Gordon_(writer) "Thomas Gordon (writer)"), and [Benjamin Hoadly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Hoadly "Benjamin Hoadly"), whose political ideas in turn also had a strong influence on the American Patriots.[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-135) His work also inspired symbols used in the American Revolution such as the "Appeal to Heaven" found on the [Pine Tree Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_Tree_Flag "Pine Tree Flag"), which alludes to Locke's concept of the [right of revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_revolution "Right of revolution").[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-136)

The theory of the social contract influenced the belief among many of the Founders that the [right of the people to overthrow their leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_revolution "Right of revolution"), should those leaders betray the historic [rights of Englishmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_of_Englishmen "Rights of Englishmen"), was one of the "natural rights" of man.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Toth1989-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-Toth1989)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cohen2008-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-Cohen2008) The Americans heavily relied on [Montesquieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Secondat,_baron_de_Montesquieu "Charles de Secondat, baron de Montesquieu")'s analysis of the wisdom of the "balanced" British Constitution ( [mixed government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_government "Mixed government")) in writing the state and national constitutions.

#### Republicanism

Main article: [Republicanism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States "Republicanism in the United States")

The American interpretation of [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism "Republicanism") was inspired by the [Whig party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whig_(British_political_party) "Whig (British political party)") in Great Britain which openly criticized the corruption within the British government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weintraub2005-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-Weintraub2005) Americans were increasingly embracing republican values, seeing Britain as corrupt and hostile to American interests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-140) [Radical Whig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_Whig "Radical Whig") ideology profoundly influenced colonial American political philosophy with its love of liberty and opposition to tyrannical government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200551-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMiddlekauff200551) The American revolutionaries associated political corruption with ostentatious luxury and inherited aristocracy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-142)

The Founding Fathers were strong advocates of republican values, particularly [Samuel Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Adams "Samuel Adams"), [Patrick Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Henry "Patrick Henry"), [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams "John Adams"), [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin"), [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson "Thomas Jefferson"), [Thomas Paine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Paine "Thomas Paine"), [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington"), [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison "James Madison"), and [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton "Alexander Hamilton"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-143) which required men to put civic duty ahead of their personal desires. Men were [honor bound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honour "Honour") by civic obligation to be prepared and willing to fight for the rights and liberties of their countrymen. John Adams wrote to [Mercy Otis Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercy_Otis_Warren "Mercy Otis Warren") in 1776, agreeing with some classical Greek and Roman thinkers: "Public Virtue cannot exist without private, and public Virtue is the only Foundation of Republics." He continued:

> There must be a positive Passion for the public good, the public Interest, Honour, Power, and Glory, established in the Minds of the People, or there can be no Republican Government, nor any real Liberty. And this public Passion must be Superior to all private Passions. Men must be ready, they must pride themselves, and be happy to sacrifice their private Pleasures, Passions, and Interests, nay their private Friendships and dearest connections, when they Stand in Competition with the Rights of society.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Rahe1994-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-Rahe1994)

#### Protestant dissenters and the Great Awakening

Main articles: [English Dissenters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Dissenters "English Dissenters") and [First Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Great_Awakening "First Great Awakening")

See also: [List of clergy in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_clergy_in_the_American_Revolution "List of clergy in the American Revolution") and [Quakers in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers_in_the_American_Revolution "Quakers in the American Revolution")

Protestant churches that had separated from the [Church of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_England "Church of England"), called "dissenters", were the "school of democracy", in the words of historian Patricia Bonomi.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bonomi-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-Bonomi) Before the Revolution, the [Southern Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Colonies "Southern Colonies") and three of the [New England Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Colonies "New England Colonies") had official legislatively [established churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Established_church "Established church"): [Congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregationalism_in_the_United_States "Congregationalism in the United States") in [Massachusetts Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Massachusetts_Bay "Province of Massachusetts Bay"), [Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Colony "Connecticut Colony"), and [New Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Hampshire "Province of New Hampshire"), and the Church of England in [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Maryland "Province of Maryland"), [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia "Colony of Virginia"), [North-Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_North-Carolina "Province of North-Carolina"), [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_South_Carolina "Province of South Carolina"), and [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Georgia "Province of Georgia"). The colonies of [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_York "Province of New York"), [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey "Province of New Jersey"), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Pennsylvania "Province of Pennsylvania"), [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_Colony "Delaware Colony"), and the [Colony of Rhode Island and Providence Plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Rhode_Island_and_Providence_Plantations "Colony of Rhode Island and Providence Plantations") had no officially established churches.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Colonial_America-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-Colonial_America) Church membership statistics from the period are unreliable and scarce,[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-147) but what little data exists indicates that the Church of England was not in the majority, not even in the colonies where it was the established church, and they probably did not comprise even 30 percent of the population in most localities (with the possible exception of Virginia).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Colonial_America-1)[\[143\]](#refnote-Colonial_America)

[John Witherspoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Witherspoon "John Witherspoon"), who was considered a ["new light" Presbyterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_and_New_Lights "Old and New Lights"), wrote widely circulated sermons linking the American Revolution to the teachings of the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible "Bible"). Throughout the colonies, dissenting ministers from the Congregational, [Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists "Baptists"), and Presbyterian churches preached Revolutionary themes in their sermons while most Church of England clergymen preached loyalty to the king, the [titular head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Governor_of_the_Church_of_England "Supreme Governor of the Church of England") of the English [state church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_church "State church").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nelson1961-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-Nelson1961) Religious motivation for fighting tyranny transcended socioeconomic lines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bonomi-1)[\[142\]](#refnote-Bonomi) The Declaration of Independence also referred to the "Laws of Nature and of Nature's God" as justification for the Revolution: the signers of the Declaration professed their "firm reliance on the Protection of divine Providence", and they appealed to "the Supreme Judge for the rectitude of our intentions".[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-149)

Historian [Bernard Bailyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Bailyn "Bernard Bailyn") argues that the evangelicalism of the era challenged traditional notions of natural hierarchy by preaching that the Bible teaches all men are equal, and the true value of a man therefore lies in his moral behavior, not in his class.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-150) Kidd argues that religious [disestablishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disestablishmentarianism "Disestablishmentarianism"), belief in God as the source of human rights, and shared convictions about sin, virtue, and divine providence, worked together to unite rationalists and evangelicals and thus encouraged a large proportion of Americans to fight for sovereignty outside the Empire. Bailyn, on the other hand, denies that religion played such a critical role.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Thomas_S._Kidd_2010-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-Thomas_S._Kidd_2010) Alan Heimert argues that New Light anti-authoritarianism was essential to furthering democracy in colonial American society, and set the stage for a confrontation with British monarchical and aristocratic rule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-152)

### Class and psychology of the factions

Main article: [Political culture of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_culture_of_the_United_States "Political culture of the United States")

Further information: [Social class in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_the_United_States "Social class in the United States") and [Culture of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States "Culture of the United States")

Historians in the early 20th century such as [J. Franklin Jameson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Franklin_Jameson "J. Franklin Jameson") examined the class composition of the Patriot cause, looking for evidence of a class war inside the revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-153) More recent historians have largely abandoned that interpretation, emphasizing instead the high level of ideological unity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-154) Both Loyalists and Patriots were a "mixed lot",[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nash_2005-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-Nash_2005)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Resch_2006-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-Resch_2006) but ideological demands always came first.

#### King George III

Main article: [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III")

See also: [Monarchy of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_the_United_Kingdom "Monarchy of the United Kingdom") and [Royal prerogative in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative_in_the_United_Kingdom "Royal prerogative in the United Kingdom")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Portrait_of_George_III_by_Johann_Heinrich_von_Hurter.jpg/250px-Portrait_of_George_III_by_Johann_Heinrich_von_Hurter.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Portrait_of_George_III_by_Johann_Heinrich_von_Hurter.jpg) King [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III") depicted in a 1781 portrait

The revolution became a personal issue for [the king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III "George III"), fueled by his growing belief that British leniency would be taken as weakness by the Americans. He also sincerely believed that he was defending [Britain's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_constitution "British constitution") against usurpers, rather than opposing patriots fighting for their natural rights.[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-157) King George III is often accused of obstinately trying to keep Great Britain at war with the revolutionaries in America, despite the opinions of his own ministers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-158) In the words of the British historian [George Otto Trevelyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_George_Trevelyan,_2nd_Baronet "Sir George Trevelyan, 2nd Baronet"), the King was determined "never to acknowledge the independence of the Americans, and to punish their contumacy by the indefinite prolongation of a war which promised to be eternal."[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-159) The king wanted to "keep the rebels harassed, anxious, and poor, until the day when, by a natural and inevitable process, discontent and disappointment were converted into penitence and remorse".[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-160) Later historians defend George by saying in the context of the times no king would willingly surrender such a large territory,[&#8202;](#ref-back-dnb-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-dnb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cg-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-cg) and his conduct was far less ruthless than contemporary monarchs in Europe.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-163) After the surrender of a British army at Saratoga, both Parliament and the British people were largely in favor of the war; recruitment ran at high levels and although political opponents were vocal, they remained a small minority.[&#8202;](#ref-back-dnb-1)[\[158\]](#refnote-dnb)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-164)

With the early setbacks in America, [Lord North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_North "Lord North") asked to transfer power to [Lord Chatham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Chatham "Lord Chatham"), whom he thought more capable, but George refused to do so; he suggested instead that Chatham serve as a subordinate minister in North's administration, but Chatham refused. He died later in the same year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-165) Lord North was allied to the "King's Friends" [faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_faction "Political faction") in Parliament and believed George III had the right to exercise extensive powers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-166) In early 1778, [Britain's chief rival France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_France "Early modern France") signed a [treaty of alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alliance_(1778) "Treaty of Alliance (1778)") with the United States, and the confrontation soon escalated from a "rebellion" to something that has been characterized as "world war".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willcox161165-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-Willcox161165) The French fleet was able to outrun the British naval blockade of the Mediterranean and sailed to North America.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willcox161165-1)[\[164\]](#refnote-Willcox161165) The conflict now affected North America, Europe and [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_India "Colonial India").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Willcox161165-2)[\[164\]](#refnote-Willcox161165) The United States and France were joined by [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Spain "Enlightenment in Spain") in 1779 and the [Dutch Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic "Dutch Republic"), while Britain had no major allies of its own, except for the Loyalist minority in America and German auxiliaries (i.e. _Hessians_). [Lord Gower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granville_Leveson-Gower,_1st_Marquess_of_Stafford "Granville Leveson-Gower, 1st Marquess of Stafford") and [Lord Weymouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Thynne,_1st_Marquess_of_Bath "Thomas Thynne, 1st Marquess of Bath") both resigned from the government. Lord North again requested that he also be allowed to resign, but he stayed in office at George III's insistence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-:0) Opposition to the costly war was increasing, and in June 1780 contributed to disturbances in London known as the [Gordon riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_riots "Gordon riots").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[165\]](#refnote-:0)

As late as the [Siege of Charleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Charleston "Siege of Charleston") in 1780, Loyalists could still believe in their eventual victory, as British troops inflicted defeats on the Continental forces at the [Battle of Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Camden "Battle of Camden") and the [Battle of Guilford Court House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Guilford_Court_House "Battle of Guilford Court House").[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-169) In late 1781, the news of Cornwallis's surrender at the siege of Yorktown reached London; Lord North's parliamentary support ebbed away and he resigned the following year. The king drafted an abdication notice, which was never delivered,[&#8202;](#ref-back-cg-1)[\[159\]](#refnote-cg)[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-170) finally accepted the defeat in North America, and authorized peace negotiations. The [Treaties of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Paris_(1783) "Peace of Paris (1783)"), by which Britain recognized the sovereignty of the United States and [returned Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Florida "Spanish Florida") to Spain, were signed in 1782 and 1783 respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-171) In early 1783, George III privately conceded "America is lost!" He reflected that the former colonies had developed into Britain's "successful rivals" in commercial trade and fishing as the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-172)

When [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams "John Adams") was appointed [American Minister to London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Kingdom "United States Ambassador to the United Kingdom") in 1785, George had become resigned to the new relationship between his country and the former colonies. He told Adams, "I was the last to consent to the separation; but the separation having been made and having become inevitable, I have always said, as I say now, that I would be the first to meet the friendship of the United States as an independent power."[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-173)

#### Patriots

Main article: [Patriot (American Revolution)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_(American_Revolution) "Patriot (American Revolution)")

Further information: [Sons of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty "Sons of Liberty")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Commonsense.jpg/250px-Commonsense.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Commonsense.jpg) The U.S. motto _[Novus ordo seclorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novus_ordo_seclorum "Novus ordo seclorum")_, meaning "A New Age Now Begins", is paraphrased from [Thomas Paine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Paine "Thomas Paine")'s _[Common Sense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Sense "Common Sense")_, published January 10, 1776. "We have it in our power to begin the world over again," Paine wrote.

Those who fought for independence were called "Revolutionaries", "Continentals", "Rebels", "Patriots", "Whigs", "Congress-men", or "Americans" during and after the war. They included a full range of social and economic classes but were unanimous regarding the need to defend the natural rights of Americans and uphold the principles of republicanism in rejecting monarchy and aristocracy, while emphasizing civic virtue by citizens. The signers of the Declaration of Independence were overwhelmingly—with definite exceptions—well-educated, of British stock, and of the Protestant faith.[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-174)[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-175) [Newspapers were strongholds of patriotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_American_publishers_and_printers "Early American publishers and printers") (although there were a few Loyalist papers) and printed many pamphlets, announcements, patriotic letters, and pronouncements.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-176)

According to historian Robert Calhoon, 40 to 45 percent of the white population in the Thirteen Colonies supported the Patriots' cause, 15 to 20 percent supported the Loyalists, and the remainder were neutral or kept a low profile.[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-177) Mark Lender concludes that ordinary people became insurgents against the British because they held a sense of rights which the British were violating, rights that stressed local autonomy, fair dealing, and government by consent. They were highly sensitive to the issue of tyranny, which they saw manifested in the British response to the Boston Tea Party. The arrival in Boston of the British Army heightened their sense of violated rights, leading to rage and demands for revenge. They had faith that God was on their side.[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-178)

The Patriots viewed independence as a means to gain freedom from British oppression and initially to reassert their rights on par with subjects in Britain. Most yeomen farmers, craftsmen, and small merchants joined the Patriot cause to demand more political equality. They were especially successful in Pennsylvania but less so in New England, where John Adams attacked Thomas Paine's _Common Sense_ for the "absurd democratical notions" that it proposed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nash_2005-1)[\[152\]](#refnote-Nash_2005)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Resch_2006-1)[\[153\]](#refnote-Resch_2006)

Thomas Paine published his pamphlet [_Common Sense_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Sense_(pamphlet) "Common Sense (pamphlet)") in January 1776, after the Revolution had started. It was widely distributed and often read aloud in taverns, contributing significantly to concurrently spreading the ideas of republicanism and liberalism, bolstering enthusiasm for separation from Great Britain, and encouraging recruitment for the Continental Army.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ferguson,_2000_pp._465-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-Ferguson,_2000_pp._465) Paine presented the Revolution as the solution for Americans alarmed by the threat of tyranny.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ferguson,_2000_pp._465-1)[\[176\]](#refnote-Ferguson,_2000_pp._465)

#### Loyalists

Main article: [Loyalist (American Revolution)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalist_(American_Revolution) "Loyalist (American Revolution)")

See also: [Loyalists fighting in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalists_fighting_in_the_American_Revolution "Loyalists fighting in the American Revolution") and [United Empire Loyalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Empire_Loyalist "United Empire Loyalist")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Philip_Dawe_%28attributed%29%2C_The_Bostonians_Paying_the_Excise-man%2C_or_Tarring_and_Feathering_%281774%29.jpg/250px-Philip_Dawe_%28attributed%29%2C_The_Bostonians_Paying_the_Excise-man%2C_or_Tarring_and_Feathering_%281774%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Philip_Dawe_(attributed),_The_Bostonians_Paying_the_Excise-man,_or_Tarring_and_Feathering_(1774).jpg) [Patriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriots_(American_Revolution) "Patriots (American Revolution)") [tarring and feathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarring_and_feathering "Tarring and feathering") Loyalist [John Malcolm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Malcolm_(Loyalist) "John Malcolm (Loyalist)") depicted in a 1774 painting

The consensus of scholars is that about 15 to 20 percent of the white population remained loyal to the British Crown.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-180) Those who actively supported the king were known at the time as "Loyalists", "Tories", or "King's men". The Loyalists never controlled territory unless the British Army occupied it. They were typically older, less willing to break with old loyalties, and often connected to the Church of England; they included many established merchants with strong business connections throughout the Empire, as well as royal officials such as Thomas Hutchinson of Boston.[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-181)

In the mid-20th century, historian [Leonard Woods Labaree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_Woods_Labaree "Leonard Woods Labaree") identified eight characteristics of the Loyalists that made them essentially conservative, opposite to the characteristics of the Patriots.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Labaree,_1948_pp._164-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-Labaree,_1948_pp._164) Loyalists tended to feel that resistance to the Crown was morally wrong, while the Patriots thought that morality was on their side.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hull_1978_pp._344-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-Hull_1978_pp._344)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wallace,_1972_pp._167-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-Wallace,_1972_pp._167) Loyalists were alienated when the Patriots resorted to violence, such as burning houses and [tarring and feathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarring_and_feathering "Tarring and feathering"). Many Loyalists wanted to take a centrist position and resisted the Patriots' demand to declare their opposition to the Crown. Numerous Loyalists had maintained strong and long-standing relations with Britain, especially merchants in port cities such as New York and Boston.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hull_1978_pp._344-1)[\[180\]](#refnote-Hull_1978_pp._344)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wallace,_1972_pp._167-1)[\[181\]](#refnote-Wallace,_1972_pp._167) Many Loyalists felt that independence was bound to come eventually, but they were fearful that revolution might lead to anarchy, tyranny, or [mob rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mob_rule "Mob rule"). In contrast, the prevailing attitude among Patriots was a desire to seize the initiative.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hull_1978_pp._344-2)[\[180\]](#refnote-Hull_1978_pp._344)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wallace,_1972_pp._167-2)[\[181\]](#refnote-Wallace,_1972_pp._167) Labaree also contended that Loyalists were pessimists who lacked the confidence in the future displayed by the Patriots.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Labaree,_1948_pp._164-1)[\[179\]](#refnote-Labaree,_1948_pp._164)

There were "tens of thousands" of [Black Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Loyalist "Black Loyalist"), enslaved African Americans who escaped to British lines and supported Britain's cause via several means. Many of them died from disease, but the survivors were evacuated by the British to [their remaining colonies in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America "British North America"). Those enslaved by Loyalists remained in bondage[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-185)

The revolution could divide families, such as [William Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Franklin "William Franklin"), son of Benjamin Franklin and royal governor of the [Province of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey "Province of New Jersey") who remained loyal to the Crown throughout the war. He and his father never spoke again.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-186) Recent immigrants who had not been fully Americanized were also inclined to support the King.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-187)

After the war, the great majority of the half-million Loyalists remained in America and resumed normal lives. Some became prominent American leaders, such as [Samuel Seabury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Seabury_(1729%E2%80%931796) "Samuel Seabury (1729–1796)"). Approximately 46,000 Loyalists relocated to Canada; others moved to Britain (7,000), Florida, or the West Indies (9,000). The exiles represented approximately two percent of the total population of the colonies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pole_1994-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-Pole_1994) Nearly all Black Loyalists left for Nova Scotia, Florida, or England, where they could remain free.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-189) Loyalists who left the South in 1783 took thousands of enslaved persons with them as they fled to the [British West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_West_Indies "British West Indies").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pole_1994-1)[\[185\]](#refnote-Pole_1994)

#### Neutrals

See also: [Quakers in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers_in_the_American_Revolution "Quakers in the American Revolution")

A minority of uncertain size tried to stay neutral in the war. Most kept a low profile, but the Quakers were the most important group to speak out for neutrality, especially in Pennsylvania. The Quakers continued to do business with the British even after the war began, and they were accused of supporting British rule, "contrivers and authors of seditious publications" critical of the revolutionary cause.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-190)\[ _[full citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#What_information_to_include "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] Most Quakers remained neutral, although [a sizeable number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers_in_the_American_Revolution "Quakers in the American Revolution") participated to some degree.

#### Roles of women

Main article: [Women in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_American_Revolution "Women in the American Revolution")

See also: [Republican motherhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_motherhood "Republican motherhood")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Mrs_James_Warren_%28Mercy_Otis%29%2C_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg/250px-Mrs_James_Warren_%28Mercy_Otis%29%2C_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mrs_James_Warren_(Mercy_Otis),_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg) [Mercy Otis Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercy_Otis_Warren "Mercy Otis Warren") published poems and plays that attacked royal authority and urged colonists to resist British rule.

Women contributed to the American Revolution in many ways and were involved on both sides. Formal politics did not include women, but ordinary domestic behaviors became charged with political significance as Patriot women confronted a war which permeated all aspects of political, civil, and domestic life. They participated by boycotting British goods, spying on the British, following armies as they marched, washing, cooking, and mending for soldiers, delivering secret messages, and even fighting disguised as men in a few cases, such as [Deborah Samson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deborah_Samson "Deborah Samson"). [Mercy Otis Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercy_Otis_Warren "Mercy Otis Warren") held meetings in her house and cleverly attacked Loyalists with her creative plays and histories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-191) Many women also acted as nurses and helpers, tending to the soldiers' wounds and buying and selling goods for them. Some of these [camp followers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_followers "Camp followers") even participated in combat, such as Madam John Turchin who led her husband's regiment into battle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-192) Above all, women continued the agricultural work at home to feed their families and the armies. They maintained their families during their husbands' absences and sometimes after their deaths.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berkin,_2006_pp._59–60-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-Berkin,_2006_pp._59–60)

American women were integral to the success of the boycott of British goods,[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-194) as the boycotted items were largely household articles such as tea and cloth. Women had to return to knitting goods and to spinning and weaving their own cloth—skills that had fallen into disuse. In 1769, the women of Boston produced 40,000 skeins of yarn, and 180 women in [Middletown, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middleton,_Massachusetts "Middleton, Massachusetts") wove 20,522 yards (18,765m) of cloth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berkin,_2006_pp._59–60-1)[\[190\]](#refnote-Berkin,_2006_pp._59–60) Many women gathered food, money, clothes, and other supplies during the war to help the soldiers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-195) A woman's loyalty to her husband could become an open political act, especially for women in America committed to men who remained loyal to the King. Legal divorce, usually rare, was granted to Patriot women whose husbands supported the King.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-197)

### Other participants

Further information: [Diplomacy in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomacy_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Diplomacy in the American Revolutionary War")

#### France and Spain

Main articles: [France in the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "France in the American Revolutionary War") and [Spain and the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_and_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Spain and the American Revolutionary War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/LouisXVI-France1.jpg/250px-LouisXVI-France1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LouisXVI-France1.jpg) [Louis XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XVI "Louis XVI"), King of France and Navarre

In early 1776, France set up a major program of aid to the Americans, and the Spanish secretly added funds. Each country spent one million "livres tournaises" to buy munitions. A [dummy corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dummy_corporation "Dummy corporation") run by [Pierre Beaumarchais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Beaumarchais "Pierre Beaumarchais") concealed their activities. American Patriots obtained some munitions from the Dutch Republic as well, through the French and Spanish ports in the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies "West Indies").[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-198) Heavy expenditures and a weak taxation system pushed France toward bankruptcy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-199)

In 1777, [Charles François Adrien le Paulmier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Fran%C3%A7ois_Adrien_le_Paulmier "Charles François Adrien le Paulmier"), Chevalier d'Annemours, acting as a [secret agent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espionage "Espionage") for France, made sure General [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington") was privy to his mission. He followed Congress around for the next two years, reporting what he observed back to France.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-200) The [Treaty of Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alliance_(1778) "Treaty of Alliance (1778)") between the French and the Americans followed in 1778, which led to more French money, [matériel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mat%C3%A9riel "Matériel") and troops being sent to the United States.

Spain did not officially recognize the United States, but it was a French ally and it separately declared war on Britain on June 21, 1779. [Bernardo de Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_de_G%C3%A1lvez "Bernardo de Gálvez"), general of the Spanish forces in [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain "New Spain") bordering the Americans, also served as governor of Louisiana. He led an expedition of colonial troops to capture Florida from the British and to keep open a vital conduit for supplies going to the Americans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-201)

#### Germans

Main articles: [Germans in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germans_in_the_American_Revolution "Germans in the American Revolution") and [Hessian (soldier)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hessian_(soldier) "Hessian (soldier)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Friedrich_Wilhelm_von_Steuben_by_Ralph_Earl.jpg/250px-Friedrich_Wilhelm_von_Steuben_by_Ralph_Earl.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Friedrich_Wilhelm_von_Steuben_by_Ralph_Earl.jpg) [Friedrich Wilhelm von Steuben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Wilhelm_von_Steuben "Friedrich Wilhelm von Steuben") was a former [Prussian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussian_Army "Prussian Army") officer who served as [inspector general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Inspector_General_of_the_United_States_Army "Office of the Inspector General of the United States Army") of the Continental Army during the American Revolutionary War. He is credited with teaching the Continental Army [the essentials of military drill and discipline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulations_for_the_Order_and_Discipline_of_the_Troops_of_the_United_States "Regulations for the Order and Discipline of the Troops of the United States") beginning at [Valley Forge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_Forge "Valley Forge") in 1778, considered a turning point for the Americans.

Ethnic Germans served on both sides of the American Revolutionary War. As George III was also the [Elector of Hanover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elector_of_Hanover "Elector of Hanover"), many supported the Loyalist cause and served as allies of the [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain "Kingdom of Great Britain"); most notably rented [auxiliary troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auxiliaries "Auxiliaries")[&#8202;](#ref-back-atwood-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-atwood) from German states such as the [Landgraviate of Hessen-Kassel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landgraviate_of_Hessen-Kassel "Landgraviate of Hessen-Kassel").

American Patriots tended to represent such troops as [mercenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercenary "Mercenary") in propaganda against the British Crown. Even American historians followed suit, in spite of Colonial-era jurists drawing a distinction between auxiliaries and mercenaries, with auxiliaries serving their prince when sent to the aid of another prince, and mercenaries serving a foreign prince as individuals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-atwood-1)[\[199\]](#refnote-atwood) By this distinction the troops which served in the American Revolution were auxiliaries.

Other German individuals came to assist the American revolutionaries, most notably [Friedrich Wilhelm von Steuben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Wilhelm_von_Steuben "Friedrich Wilhelm von Steuben"), who served as a general in the Continental Army and is credited with professionalizing that force, but most Germans who served were already colonists.
