Von Steuben's native Prussia joined the [League of Armed Neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_League_of_Armed_Neutrality "First League of Armed Neutrality"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECommager1958994-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECommager1958994) and King [Frederick II of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_the_Great "Frederick the Great") was well appreciated in the United States for his support early in the war. He expressed interest in opening trade with the United States and bypassing English ports, and allowed an American agent to buy arms in Prussia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERosengarten19065-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERosengarten19065) Frederick predicted American success,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERosengarten190613-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERosengarten190613) and promised to recognize the United States and American diplomats once France did the same.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERosengarten190614-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERosengarten190614) Prussia also interfered in the recruiting efforts of Russia and neighboring German states when they raised armies to send to the Americas, and Frederick II forbade enlistment for the American war within Prussia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERosengarten188622-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERosengarten188622) All Prussian roads were denied to troops from Anhalt-Zerbst,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELowell188450-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELowell188450) which delayed reinforcements that Howe had hoped to receive during the winter of 1777–1778.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERosengarten190617-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERosengarten190617)

However, when the [War of the Bavarian Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Bavarian_Succession "War of the Bavarian Succession") (1778–1779) erupted, Frederick II became much more cautious with Prussian/British relations. U.S. ships were denied access to Prussian ports, and Frederick refused to officially recognize the United States until they had signed the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1783) "Treaty of Paris (1783)"). Even after the war, Frederick II predicted that the United States was too large to operate as a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic "Republic"), and that it would soon rejoin the British Empire with representatives in Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERosengarten190619-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERosengarten190619)

#### Native Americans

Main article: [Native Americans in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States "Native Americans in the United States")

Further information: [Western theater of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War "Western theater of the American Revolutionary War"), [Northern theater of the American Revolutionary War after Saratoga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War_after_Saratoga "Northern theater of the American Revolutionary War after Saratoga"), [Treaty of Fort Pitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Fort_Pitt "Treaty of Fort Pitt"), and [Haudenosaunee Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haudenosaunee_Confederacy "Haudenosaunee Confederacy")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Joseph_Brant_painting_by_George_Romney_1776_%282%29.jpg/250px-Joseph_Brant_painting_by_George_Romney_1776_%282%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Joseph_Brant_painting_by_George_Romney_1776_(2).jpg) [Thayendanegea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Brant "Joseph Brant"), a [Mohawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohawk_people "Mohawk people") military and political leader, was the most prominent indigenous leader opposing the Patriot forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cornelison-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-Cornelison)

Most Indigenous people rejected pleas that they remain neutral and instead supported the British Crown. The great majority of the ~200,000 indigenous people east of the Mississippi distrusted the Americans and supported the British cause, hoping to forestall continued expansion of settlement into their territories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-212)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECalloway1995-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECalloway1995) Those tribes closely involved in trade tended to side with the Patriots, although political factors were important as well. Some tried to remain neutral, seeing little value in joining what they perceived to be a "white man's war", and fearing reprisals from whichever side they opposed.

The great majority of indigenous people did not participate directly in the war, with the notable exceptions of warriors and bands associated with four of the [Haudenosaunee Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquois "Iroquois") ( _also_ Iroquois) tribes in New York and Pennsylvania which allied with the British,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECalloway1995-1)[\[210\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECalloway1995) and the [Oneida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oneida_people "Oneida people") and [Tuscarora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuscarora_people "Tuscarora people") tribes among the Iroquois of central and western New York who supported the American cause.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-214) The British did have other indigenous allies, particularly in the [regions of southwest Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_(1763%E2%80%931791) "Province of Quebec (1763–1791)") on the Patriot's frontier. The British provided arms to Indigenous people who were led by Loyalists in war parties to raid frontier settlements from the [Carolinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Carolina "Province of Carolina") to New York. These war parties killed many settlers on the frontier, especially in western Pennsylvania and New York's Mohawk Valley.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-215)

In 1776, [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee "Cherokee") war parties attacked American settlers all along the southern Quebec frontier of the uplands throughout the [Washington District, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_District,_North_Carolina "Washington District, North Carolina") (now Tennessee) and the Kentucky wilderness area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-216) The [Chickamauga Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickamauga_Cherokee "Chickamauga Cherokee") under [Tsiyu Gansini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragging_Canoe "Dragging Canoe") ( _also_ Dragging Canoe) allied themselves closely with the British, and fought on for an additional decade after the Treaty of Paris was signed. They launched raids with roughly 200 warriors, as seen in the [Cherokee–American wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee%E2%80%93American_wars "Cherokee–American wars"); they could not mobilize enough forces to invade settler areas without the help of allies, most often the [Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscogee "Muscogee").

[Joseph Brant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Brant "Joseph Brant") ( _also_ Thayendanegea), from the powerful [Mohawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohawk_people "Mohawk people") tribe of the Haudenosaunee Confederacy, was the most prominent Indigenous leader against the Patriot forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cornelison-1)[\[208\]](#refnote-Cornelison) In 1778 and 1780, he led 300 Iroquois warriors and 100 white Loyalists in multiple attacks on small frontier settlements in New York and Pennsylvania, killing many settlers and destroying villages, crops, and food stockpiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-217)

In 1779, the [Continental Army forced the hostile Indigenous people out of upstate New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sullivan_Expedition "Sullivan Expedition") when Washington sent an army under [John Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Sullivan_(general) "John Sullivan (general)") which destroyed 40 evacuated Iroquois villages in central and western New York. The [Battle of Newtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Newtown "Battle of Newtown") proved decisive, as the Patriots had an advantage of three-to-one, and it ended significant resistance; there was little combat otherwise. Facing starvation and homeless for the winter, the Iroquois that allied with the British fled to Canada.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-218)

At the peace conference following the war, the British ceded indigenous lands without consultation with their allies that inhabited them. They transferred control to the United States of all the land south of the Great Lakes, east of the Mississippi, and north of Florida. Calloway concludes:

> Burned villages and crops, murdered chiefs, divided councils and civil wars, migrations, towns and forts choked with refugees, economic disruption, breaking of ancient traditions, losses in battle and to disease and hunger, betrayal to their enemies, all made the American Revolution one of the darkest periods in American Indian history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECalloway1995290-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECalloway1995290)

#### Black Americans

Main article: [African Americans in the Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans_in_the_Revolutionary_War "African Americans in the Revolutionary War")

Further information: [Black Patriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Patriot "Black Patriot"), [Black Loyalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Loyalist "Black Loyalist"), and [Book of Negroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Negroes "Book of Negroes")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/%22Crispus_Attucks%2C%22_by_Herschel_Levit%2C_mural_at_the_Recorder_of_Deeds_building%2C_built_in_1943._515_D_St.%2C_NW%2C_Washington%2C_D.C_LCCN2010641712.tif/lossy-page1-250px-%22Crispus_Attucks%2C%22_by_Herschel_Levit%2C_mural_at_the_Recorder_of_Deeds_building%2C_built_in_1943._515_D_St.%2C_NW%2C_Washington%2C_D.C_LCCN2010641712.tif.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%22Crispus_Attucks,%22_by_Herschel_Levit,_mural_at_the_Recorder_of_Deeds_building,_built_in_1943._515_D_St.,_NW,_Washington,_D.C_LCCN2010641712.tif) _Crispus Attucks_, a (c.1943) portrait by [Herschel Levit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herschel_Levit "Herschel Levit") depicts [Attucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crispus_Attucks "Crispus Attucks"), who is considered to be the first American to die for the cause of independence in the Revolution.

The existence of [slavery in the American colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_colonial_United_States "Slavery in the colonial United States") had attracted criticism from both sides of the Atlantic as many could not reconcile the existence of the institution with the egalitarian ideals espoused by leaders of the Revolution. In a text opposing the grievances of the colonists, the English writer [Samuel Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Johnson "Samuel Johnson") wrote: "how is it we hear the loudest yelps for liberty among the drivers of the [Negroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negro "Negro")?".[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-220) Referring to this contradiction, the English writer and abolitionist [Thomas Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Day_(writer) "Thomas Day (writer)") wrote in a 1776 letter that:

> if there be an object truly ridiculous in nature, it is an American patriot, signing resolutions of independency with the one hand, and with the other brandishing a whip over his affrighted slaves.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-221)

[Crispus Attucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crispus_Attucks "Crispus Attucks"), of mixed racial heritage, was one of the five people killed in the [Boston Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Massacre "Boston Massacre") in 1770 and is considered the first American casualty for the cause of independence. Free Blacks in the [New England Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Colonies "New England Colonies") and [Middle Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Colonies "Middle Colonies") in the North as well as [Southern Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Colonies "Southern Colonies") fought on both sides of the War, but the majority fought for the Patriots. Gary Nash reports that there were about 9,000 Black veteran Patriots, counting the Continental Army and Navy, state militia units, privateers, wagoneers in the Army, servants to officers, and spies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-222) [Ray Raphael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Raphael "Ray Raphael") notes that thousands did join the Loyalist cause, but "a far larger number, free as well as slave, tried to further their interests by siding with the patriots."[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-223)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Soldiers_at_the_siege_of_Yorktown_%281781%29%2C_by_Jean-Baptiste-Antoine_DeVerger_%28cropped%29.png/250px-Soldiers_at_the_siege_of_Yorktown_%281781%29%2C_by_Jean-Baptiste-Antoine_DeVerger_%28cropped%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Soldiers_at_the_siege_of_Yorktown_(1781),_by_Jean-Baptiste-Antoine_DeVerger_(cropped).png) A Black soldier of the [1st Rhode Island Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Rhode_Island_Regiment "1st Rhode Island Regiment"), widely regarded as the first Black battalion in [U.S. military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Armed_Forces "United States Armed Forces") history

The effects of the war were more dramatic in the South. Tens of thousands of enslaved persons escaped to British lines throughout the South, causing dramatic losses to slaveholders and disrupting cultivation and harvesting of crops. For instance, [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina "South Carolina") was estimated to have lost about 25,000 enslaved persons to flight, migration, or death which amounted to a third of its enslaved population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-224) As noted by the historian Karen Cook Bell, during the Revolutionary War roughly one-third of all the escaped enslaved were women, even though prior to 1775 nearly 87% had been men.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-225)

During the war, British commanders attempted to weaken the Patriots by issuing proclamations of freedom to their enslaved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Revolutionary_War:_The_Home_Front-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-Revolutionary_War:_The_Home_Front) In the November 1775 document known as [Dunmore's Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunmore's_Proclamation "Dunmore's Proclamation") Virginia royal governor, [Lord Dunmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Murray,_4th_Earl_of_Dunmore "John Murray, 4th Earl of Dunmore") recruited Black men for British military service with the promise of freedom, protection for their families, and land grants. Hundreds of Black men responded and were formed into the [Royal Ethiopian Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Ethiopian_Regiment "Royal Ethiopian Regiment"). However, as noted by the historian James Corbett David, many Black people felt unable or unwilling to respond to these offers of freedom:

> Under... discouraging circumstances, the vast majority of slaves were either unable or unwilling to take their chances with the British. Those who opted not to run (many never really had a choice) may have watched events unfold with a growing sense of vindication, for the slaves who did strike out for freedom, while exhibiting remarkable courage and ingenuity in the process, had an exceptionally hard road ahead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-227)

Colonists, primarily Southern Patriots, were deeply angered by Dunmore's proclamation, which spread through all of the Thirteen Colonies and threatened to upturn the existing American racial hierarchy; it also played a major role in Southern support for American independence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-228) Accusations that the British were encouraging slave rebellions were prominent features of Patriot propaganda, with Jefferson even accusing George III in the [27 colonial grievances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/27_colonial_grievances "27 colonial grievances") of having "excited domestic Insurrections among us". The historian [Simon Schama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Schama "Simon Schama") argued that for Southern Patriots, "Theirs was a revolution, first and foremost, mobilized to protect slavery."[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-229)

The 1779 [Philipsburg Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philipsburg_Proclamation "Philipsburg Proclamation") expanded the promise of freedom for Black men who enlisted in the British military to all the colonies in rebellion. At the conclusion of the war, British forces gave transportation to 10,000 of the formerly enslaved when they evacuated [Savannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia "Savannah, Georgia") and [Charles Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina "Charleston, South Carolina"), carrying through on their promise.[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-230) They evacuated and resettled more than 3,000 [Black Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Loyalist "Black Loyalist") from New York to [Nova Scotia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Scotia "Nova Scotia"), [Upper Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Canada "Upper Canada"), and [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada "Lower Canada"). Others sailed with the British to England or were resettled as freedmen in the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies "West Indies") of the Caribbean. But the enslaved carried to the Caribbean under control of Loyalist masters generally remained enslaved until the British abolition of slavery in its colonies in 1833–1838.

More than 1,200 of the Black Loyalists of Nova Scotia later resettled in the British colony of [Sierra Leone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone "Sierra Leone") in Africa, where they became leaders of the [Krio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone_Creole_people "Sierra Leone Creole people") ethnic group of [Freetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freetown "Freetown") and the later national government. Many of their descendants still live in Sierra Leone, as well as other African countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-231)\[ _[full citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#What_information_to_include "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\] Despite the turmoil of the period, African-Americans contributed to the foundation of an American national identity during the Revolution. [Phyllis Wheatley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyllis_Wheatley "Phyllis Wheatley"), an African American poet, popularized the image of [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_(name) "Columbia (name)") to represent America.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-232)

## Effects of the revolution

Main articles: [American nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_nationalism "American nationalism") and [American civil religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_civil_religion "American civil religion")

After the Revolution, genuinely democratic politics became possible in the former American colonies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-233) The rights of the people were incorporated into state constitutions. Concepts of liberty, consent of the governed, individual rights, equality and due process under the law, and intolerance of corruption became incorporated as core values of liberal republicanism. The new United States government was empowered to undertake its own project of [territorial expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionism "Expansionism") and [settler colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler_colonialism "Settler colonialism"). The greatest challenge to the old order in Europe was the challenge to inherited political power and the democratic idea that government rests on the [consent of the governed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed "Consent of the governed"). The example of the first successful revolution against a European empire, and the first successful establishment of a republican form of democratically elected government without monarchy or any inherited political authority, provided a model for many other colonial peoples who realized that they too could break away and become self-governing nations with directly elected representative government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-234)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\]

Losing the war and the Thirteen Colonies was a shock to Britain. The war revealed the limitations of Britain's [fiscal-military state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal-military_state "Fiscal-military state") when they discovered that they suddenly faced powerful enemies with no allies, and they were dependent on extended and vulnerable transatlantic lines of communication. The defeat heightened dissension and escalated political antagonism to the king's ministers. The king went so far as to draft letters of abdication, although they were never delivered.[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-235) Inside Parliament, the primary concern changed from fears of an over-mighty monarch to the issues of representation, parliamentary reform, and government retrenchment. Unable to accept loss of the war and the colonies as possibly due to anything else but widespread [institutional corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_corruption "Institutional corruption"), reformers sought to root it out, and the result was a crisis from 1776 to 1783. The crisis ended after 1784 confidence in the British constitution was restored during the administration of Prime Minister [William Pitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger "William Pitt the Younger").[&#8202;](#ref-back-William_Hague_2004-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-William_Hague_2004)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jeremy_Black_2006-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-Jeremy_Black_2006)[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[d\]](#refnote-239)

### Interpretations

John Adams concluded in 1818:

> The Revolution was effected before the war commenced. The Revolution was in the minds and hearts of the people.... This radical change in the principles, opinions, sentiments, and affections of the people was the real American Revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-240)

The American Revolution ended an age of monarchy, and it began a new age of freedom. As a result of the growing wave started by the Revolution, there are now more people around the world living in freedom than ever before, both in absolute numbers and as a percentage of the world's population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-McDonald,_Forrest_pp._6-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-McDonald,_Forrest_pp._6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-242)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Loon,_Hendrik_p._333-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-Loon,_Hendrik_p._333)[&#8202;](#ref-back-freedomhouse-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-freedomhouse) Interpretations vary concerning the effect of the Revolution. Historians such as [Bernard Bailyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Bailyn "Bernard Bailyn"), [Gordon Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_S._Wood "Gordon S. Wood"), and [Edmund Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Morgan_(historian) "Edmund Morgan (historian)") view it as a unique and radical event which produced deep changes and had a profound effect on world affairs, such as an increasing belief in the principles of [the Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Enlightenment "The Enlightenment"). These were demonstrated by a leadership and government that espoused protection of natural rights, and a system of laws chosen by the people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-245) John Murrin, by contrast, argues that the definition of "the people" at that time was mostly restricted to free men who passed a property qualification.[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-246)[&#8202;](#ref-back-U.S._Voting_Rights-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-U.S._Voting_Rights)

Gordon Wood states:

> The American Revolution was integral to the changes occurring in American society, politics and culture.... These changes were radical, and they were extensive.... The Revolution not only radically changed the personal and social relationships of people, including the position of women, but also destroyed aristocracy as it'd been understood in the Western world for at least two millennia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-248)

Edmund Morgan has argued that, in terms of long-term impact on American society and values:

> The Revolution did revolutionize social relations. It did displace the deference, the patronage, the social divisions that had determined the way people viewed one another for centuries and still view one another in much of the world. It did give to ordinary people a pride and power, not to say an arrogance, that have continued to shock visitors from less favored lands. It may have left standing a host of inequalities that have troubled us ever since. But it generated the egalitarian view of human society that makes them troubling and makes our world so different from the one in which the revolutionists had grown up.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-249)

[Leopold von Ranke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopold_von_Ranke "Leopold von Ranke"), a leading German historian, claimed in 1848 that revolutionary American republicanism played a crucial role in the development of European liberalism:

> By abandoning English constitutionalism and creating a new republic based on [the rights of the individual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law "Natural law"), the North Americans introduced a new force in the world. Ideas spread most rapidly when they have found adequate concrete expression. Thus republicanism entered our Romanic/Germanic world... Up to this point, the conviction had prevailed in Europe that monarchy best served the interests of the nation. Now the idea spread that the nation should govern itself. But only after a state had actually been formed on the basis of [the theory of representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed "Consent of the governed") did the full significance of this idea become clear. All later revolutionary movements have this same goal... This was the complete reversal of a principle. Until then, [a king who ruled by the grace of God had been the center around which everything turned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_right_of_kings "Divine right of kings"). Now the idea emerged that [power should come from below](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_sovereignty "Popular sovereignty")... These two principles are like two opposite poles, and it is the conflict between them that determines the course of the modern world. In Europe the conflict between them had not yet taken on concrete form; with the French Revolution it did.[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-250)

### Inspiring other independence movements and revolutions

Main article: [Age of Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Revolution "Age of Revolution")

Further information: [Atlantic Revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Revolutions "Atlantic Revolutions")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Atlantic_Revolutions.png/250px-Atlantic_Revolutions.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Atlantic_Revolutions.png) The American Revolution was first of the original wave of the [Atlantic Revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Revolutions "Atlantic Revolutions"), an 18th and 19th century [revolutionary wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_wave "Revolutionary wave") in the [Atlantic World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_World "Atlantic World").

The first shot of the American Revolution at the Battle of Lexington and Concord is referred to as the ["shot heard 'round the world"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shot_heard_round_the_world "Shot heard round the world"). The Revolutionary War victory not only established the United States as the first nation founded on the consent of the governed, equality under the law, and modern constitutional republic, but marked the transition from an age of monarchy to a new age of freedom by inspiring similar movements worldwide.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134) The American Revolution was the first of the [Atlantic Revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Revolutions "Atlantic Revolutions"): followed most notably by the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution "French Revolution"), the [Haitian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution "Haitian Revolution"), and the [Latin American wars of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence "Spanish American wars of independence"). Aftershocks contributed to [rebellions in Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Rebellion_of_1798 "Irish Rebellion of 1798"), the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth "Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth"), and the Netherlands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-252)[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-253)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134-1)[\[247\]](#refnote-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134)

The [U.S. Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States "Constitution of the United States"), drafted shortly after independence, remains the world's oldest written constitution, and has been emulated by other countries, in some cases verbatim.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-254) Some historians and scholars argue that the subsequent wave of independence and revolutionary movements has contributed to the continued expansion of democratic government; 144 countries, representing two-third of the world's population, are full or partially democracies of some form.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-255)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Loon,_Hendrik_p._333-1)[\[239\]](#refnote-Loon,_Hendrik_p._333)[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-256)[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-257)[&#8202;](#ref-back-freedomhouse-1)[\[240\]](#refnote-freedomhouse)[&#8202;](#ref-back-McDonald,_Forrest_pp._6-1)[\[237\]](#refnote-McDonald,_Forrest_pp._6)

The Revolution had a strong, immediate influence in Great Britain, Ireland, the Netherlands, and France. Many British and Irish [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Whig_Party "British Whig Party") in Parliament spoke glowingly in favor of the American cause. In Ireland, the Protestant minority who controlled Ireland demanded [self-rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule "Irish Home Rule"). Under the leadership of [Henry Grattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Grattan "Henry Grattan"), the [Irish Patriot Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Patriot_Party#Grattan's_Patriots "Irish Patriot Party") forced the reversal of mercantilist prohibitions against trade with other British colonies. The King and his cabinet in London could not risk another rebellion, and so made a series of concessions to the Patriot faction in Dublin. Armed volunteer units of the _[Protestant Ascendancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Ascendancy "Protestant Ascendancy")_ were set up ostensibly to protect against an invasion from France. As had been in colonial America, so too in Ireland now the King no longer had a [monopoly of lethal force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monopoly_on_violence "Monopoly on violence"). By 1798, the Irish were [ready for a republican uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Rebellion_of_1798 "Irish Rebellion of 1798").[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-258)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134-2)[\[247\]](#refnote-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134)[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-259)

The American Declaration of Independence influenced the French [Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen "Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen") of 1789.[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-260)[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-261) The spirit of the Declaration of Independence led to laws ending slavery in all the Northern states and the Northwest Territory, with New Jersey the last in 1804. States such as New Jersey and New York adopted gradual emancipation, which kept some people as slaves for more than two decades longer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-262)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134-3)[\[247\]](#refnote-Bailyn,_Bernard_pp._35,_134)[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-263)

### Status of African Americans

Main article: [African-American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_history "African-American history")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Prince_Estabrook_memorial_close_up.jpg/250px-Prince_Estabrook_memorial_close_up.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Prince_Estabrook_memorial_close_up.jpg) A [Lexington, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexington,_Massachusetts "Lexington, Massachusetts") memorial to [Prince Estabrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Estabrook "Prince Estabrook"), who was wounded in the [Battle of Lexington and Concord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lexington_and_Concord "Battle of Lexington and Concord") and was the first Black military casualty of the American Revolutionary War[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/00SalemPoor.jpg/250px-00SalemPoor.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:00SalemPoor.jpg) A postage stamp, created at the time of the bicentennial, honors [Salem Poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salem_Poor "Salem Poor"), who was an enslaved African American man who purchased his freedom, became a soldier, and rose to fame as a war hero during the [Battle of Bunker Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bunker_Hill "Battle of Bunker Hill").[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-264)

During the revolution, the contradiction between the Patriots' professed ideals of liberty and the institution of slavery generated increased scrutiny of the latter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn):235[&#8202;](#ref-back-Moral-Capital-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-Moral-Capital):105–106[&#8202;](#ref-back-Radicalism-Wood-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-Radicalism-Wood):186 As early as 1764, the Boston Patriot leader [James Otis, Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Otis,_Jr. "James Otis, Jr.") declared that all men, "white or black", were "by the law of nature" born free.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn-1)[\[261\]](#refnote-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn):237 Anti-slavery calls became more common in the early 1770s. In 1773, [Benjamin Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Rush "Benjamin Rush"), the future signer of the Declaration of Independence, called on "advocates for American liberty" to oppose slavery.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn-2)[\[261\]](#refnote-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn):239 Slavery became an issue that had to be addressed. As historian Christopher L. Brown put it, slavery "had never been on the agenda in a serious way before," but the Revolution "forced it to be a public question from there forward."[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-268)[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-269)

In the late 1760s and early 1770s, several colonies, including Massachusetts and Virginia, attempted to restrict the slave trade, but were prevented from doing so by royally appointed governors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn-3)[\[261\]](#refnote-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn):245 In 1774, as part of a broader non-importation movement aimed at Britain, the Continental Congress called on all the colonies to ban the importation of enslaved people, and the colonies passed acts doing so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn-4)[\[261\]](#refnote-Ideological-Origins-Bailyn):245

In the first two decades after the American Revolution, state legislatures and individuals took actions to free the enslaved, in part based on Revolutionary ideals. Northern states passed new constitutions that contained language about equal rights or specifically abolished slavery; some states, such as New York and New Jersey, where slavery was more widespread, passed laws by the end of the 18th century to abolish slavery by a gradual method. By 1804, all the northern states had passed laws outlawing slavery, either immediately or over time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-270)

No southern state abolished slavery. However, enslavers could grant freedom to individuals they enslaved. Numerous slaveholders who freed their enslaved cited Revolutionary ideals in their documents; others freed their enslaved as a reward for service. Records also suggest that some slaveholders were freeing their own mixed-race children, born to enslaved mothers. The number of free Blacks as a proportion of the Black population in the upper South increased from less than 1 percent to nearly 10 percent between 1790 and 1810 as a result of these actions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[e\]](#refnote-281) Nevertheless, slavery continued in the South, where it was protected as a "peculiar institution", setting the stage for future sectional conflict between North and South over the issue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Radicalism-Wood-1)[\[263\]](#refnote-Radicalism-Wood):186–187

Thousands of free Blacks in the northern states fought in the state militias and Continental Army. In the south, both sides offered freedom to slaves who would perform military service. Roughly 20,000 enslaved persons fought in the American Revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-282)

### Status of American women

Main article: [History of women in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_women_in_the_United_States "History of women in the United States")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Betsy_Ross_1893.png/250px-Betsy_Ross_1893.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Betsy_Ross_1893.png) [Betsy Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betsy_Ross "Betsy Ross"), an American upholsterer, is allegedly known for designing the first [U.S. flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_the_United_States "Flag of the United States"), specifically known as the [Betsy Ross flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betsy_Ross_flag "Betsy Ross flag").

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| [![icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/60px-Question_book-new.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg) | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/American_Revolution "Special:EditPage/American Revolution") by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners "Help:Referencing for beginners"). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence "Wikipedia:Verifiability")._(January 2026)__([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal "Help:Maintenance template removal"))_ |

While American women were entering a period in which they found themselves gaining more identity within society, they were still very much considered to be subordinate to men, as their role remained being a good wife and mother. Having a woman who was dressed well for her role as a good wife and mother, as well as fitting the social role, was a symbol of not only status, but a family devoted to the republic. As they continued to nurture social and political partnerships, their role in enabling the success of the revolution emphasized their changing role in society – leading to the post-revolutionary reconstruction of gender ideology.

In addition, the democratic ideals of the Revolution inspired changes in the roles of women.[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-283) Patriot women married to Loyalists who left the state could get a divorce and obtain control of the ex-husband's property.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-284) [Abigail Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abigail_Adams "Abigail Adams") expressed to her husband, the president, the desire of women to have a place in the new republic:

> I desire you would remember the Ladies, and be more generous and favourable to them than your ancestors. Do not put such unlimited power into the hands of the Husbands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-285)

As discussions rose regarding the rights of man post-Revolutionary war, women began pushing for the rights of women as well. [Mary Wollstonecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Wollstonecraft "Mary Wollstonecraft") pioneered the discussion regarding women's rights, and pushed those like Abigail Adams to begin expressing the desire to want a larger place in society. Wollstonecraft was an English writer, philosopher, and advocate for women's rights who published the _Vindication of the Rights of Woman_ (1792) – challenging the idea that rights should only be granted to men. She was one of the first major advocates and foundational figures for women's rights and gender equality in a time when women were considered inferior to men. Wollstonecraft focused on equal education and social opportunities for women – believing that if women were educated the same as men, they would gain autonomy over their own lives and better contribute to society. Her ideas contributed to the conversation in allowing women to be bearers of rights alongside men. Inspired by the radical feminism in her work, women in the early republic would change their views on marriage, education, participation in public life, and autonomy. This laid the groundwork for the later women's suffrage movement, education opportunities, property rights, and more.

However, a gender hierarchy would continue to bind women's rights after the Revolution. Women in the early republic could not vote, hold political office, or earn fair wages. They also lacked opportunities for higher education and certain professions, as well as the right to own property independently of their husbands. In addition, they held little legal powers in subjects such as divorce, property rights, and child custody. A central legal concept that reinforced these restrictions was _coverture_, a central legal doctrine that limited women's lives in all aspects – making a woman's legal identity a part of their husband's. The early national period of America would continue to struggle with the concept of rights and equality.

### American Loyalist expatriation

Main article: [United Empire Loyalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Empire_Loyalist "United Empire Loyalist")

See also: [Expulsion of the Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Loyalists "Expulsion of the Loyalists")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Allegorical_painting_depicting_the_British_Empire_taking_in_American_loyalists_in_1783.jpg/250px-Allegorical_painting_depicting_the_British_Empire_taking_in_American_loyalists_in_1783.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Allegorical_painting_depicting_the_British_Empire_taking_in_American_loyalists_in_1783.jpg) [Reception of the American Loyalists by Great Britain in the Year 1783](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reception_of_the_American_Loyalists_by_Great_Britain_in_the_Year_1783 "Reception of the American Loyalists by Great Britain in the Year 1783"), a painting by early American artist [Benjamin West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_West "Benjamin West")

Tens of thousands of American Loyalists left the United States following the [war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War"); Philip Ranlet estimates 20,000, while [Maya Jasanoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Jasanoff "Maya Jasanoff") estimates as many as 70,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-286) Some migrated to Britain, but the great majority received land and subsidies for resettlement in British colonies in North America, especially [Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_(1763-1791) "Province of Quebec (1763-1791)") (concentrating in the [Eastern Townships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Townships "Eastern Townships")), [Prince Edward Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Edward_Island "Prince Edward Island"), and [Nova Scotia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Scotia "Nova Scotia").[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-287) Britain created the colonies of Upper Canada ( [Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontario "Ontario")) and [New Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Brunswick "New Brunswick") expressly for their benefit, and the Crown awarded land to Loyalists as compensation for losses in the United States. Nevertheless, approximately eighty-five percent of the Loyalists stayed in the United States as American citizens, and some of the dispersed Loyalists later returned to the U.S.[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-288) Patrick Henry spoke of the issue of allowing Loyalists to return as such: "Shall we, who have laid the proud British lion at our feet, be frightened of its whelps?" His actions helped secure return of the Loyalists to American soil.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKukla265–268-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKukla265–268)

### Commemorations

Main articles: [Commemoration of the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commemoration_of_the_American_Revolution "Commemoration of the American Revolution") and [United States Bicentennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bicentennial "United States Bicentennial")

Further information: [American Revolution Statuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution_Statuary "American Revolution Statuary") and [Independence Day (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_(United_States) "Independence Day (United States)")

See also: [Minor American Revolution holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_American_Revolution_holidays "Minor American Revolution holidays")

The American Revolution has a central place in the American memory[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-290) as the story of the nation's founding. It is covered in the schools, memorialized by two national holidays, [Washington's Birthday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Day "President's Day") in February and [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_(United_States) "Independence Day (United States)") in July, and commemorated in innumerable monuments. George Washington's estate at [Mount Vernon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Vernon "Mount Vernon") was one of the first national pilgrimages for tourists and attracted 10,000 visitors a year by the 1850s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-291)

The Revolution became a matter of contention in the 1850s in the debates leading to the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War") (1861–1865), as spokesmen of both the [Northern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_United_States "Northern United States") and the [Southern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_United_States "Southern United States") claimed that their region was the true custodian of the legacy of 1776.[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-292) The [United States Bicentennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bicentennial "United States Bicentennial") in 1976 came a year after the American withdrawal from the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War"), and speakers stressed the themes of renewal and rebirth based on a restoration of traditional values.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-293)

Today, more than 100 [battlefields and historic sites of the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:American_Revolutionary_War_sites "Category:American Revolutionary War sites") are protected and maintained by the government. The [National Park Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Park_Service "National Park Service") alone manages and maintains more than 50 battlefield parks and many other sites such as [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall "Independence Hall") that are related to the Revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-294) The private [American Battlefield Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Battlefield_Trust "American Battlefield Trust") uses government grants and other funds to preserve almost 700 acres of battlefield land in six states, and the ambitious private recreation/restoration/preservation/interpretation of over 300 acres of pre-1790 [Colonial Williamsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Williamsburg "Colonial Williamsburg") was created in the first half of the 20th century for public visitation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-295)

3. 4. 5. The Atlantic World Reborn](https://web.archive.org/web/20150211220154/http://sandbox.nyhsdev.org/sites/default/files/curriculum_materials/revolution/Revolution-Teachers-Guide.zip), explores the transformations in the world's politics from 1763 to 1815, with particular attention to three revolutions in America, France, and Haiti. Linking the attack on monarchism and aristocracy to the struggle against slavery, it at how freedom, equality, and sovereignty of the people became universal goals.
